JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Fureword.

'Again I am far, far away, and at the time of writing have not received my copy of Satur- day's "Examiner." Thus lam not in a position to comment on current local events as therein revealed. However, although lack- ing the means of inspiration and suggestion upon which I am accustomed to depend, I am at liberty to touch upon topics as a rule de- barred by want of spate.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
Volunteer Training Corps.

As I enter upon this congenial task, a com- pany of the Volunteer Training Corps go swinging smartly by on a route march. They are wearing the new uniform, and, especially for men past the recognised military age, look exceedingly well and fit. 'Nevertheless, their appearance excites ridicule in some quarters. Thoughtless jokers freely indulge in gibes at their expense, and more sober critics doubt- fully -wag their pates. Now, really, the too common attitude of discouragement in rela- tion to these manifestly earnest and self-sacri- ficing citizens is wholly unjustifiable and con- demnable; and I think that one who takes up his pen in defence of this remarkable move- ment should have no difficulty in showing that it is an entirely mistaken attitude assumed in °ignorance and short-sightedness.

An Appeal to History. When as a young fellow I was on the staff of the" Halifax Courier," that paper was edited by the late Mr. J. D. hutchinson, M.P., to whose memory newspaper proprietors may well be grateful for the substantial modifica- tion of the law of libel which he effected. His ample library included a set of " Punch " volumes, and; frequently, dipping- into these, because ,John Leech's sketches always had a peculiar fascination for me, I could not but be struck by the fact that the Volunteers in those days were constantly the butt of the Wits. That sort of thing has continued through the succeeding generations.

Our Gallant " Terriers." Even when the derided and discredited Volunteers were disbanded or merged in the Territorial system, and those who re-enlisted really became units in the British standing Army, and were no longer Volunteers in the old sense, manypeople who were not themselves willing to devote any of their leisure to mili- tary training refused to take the " Terriers " seriously. Then came the time for the per- sistent critics to hide their diminished heads. With the outbreak of war our Territorials almost to a man volunteered for foreign ser- vice, and oh the field of honour have since achieved imperishable renown. I have rela- tives and acquaintances among them, and was always proud of them. One little company alone representing a small town in which I used to live includes 'one of the oldest and one of the youngest to win the D.C.M., and both deserve the V.C.—as I think I could satisfy anyone if I had room for particulars. Historical Parallel.

Reverting to my point as to the Volunteer Training Corps, I note that the Volunteers of the mid-Victorian era to whom I have re- ferred struggled long and manfully, not only against popular prejudice and folly, with but little encouragement from the War Office. Failing for years to gain practical sympathy from the military authorities, they not only devoted their spare time to preparation for home defence, but in their enthusiasm paid for theii uniform, &c., out of their own pockets. The case of the Volunteer Training Corps is precisely on all fours with this, and War Office quidnunes are disposed to look askance at them, and at present they have to provide their own equipment—and do not make a grievance of it either, notwithstand- ing the Clamour of others for war bonus—and yet persons who are not well informed or open- eyed presume to poke fun at them. Ah, well! As it was in the beginning, etc., etc. National Registration.

To make plain, what in some quarters does not seem to be properly appreciated, that this week's registration work is not in essentials very different from an ordinary census, de- mands no such effort of memory as the fore- going. Of course, secrecy and accuracy are the main features of these great tasks. In order to ensure the latter in particular, extra- ordinary pains appear to have been taken by the Registrar General and his staff, and the resultant compilation is naturally expected to be of considerable statistical and general value. Local 'authorities and enumerators— volunteers again for the most part—have been deluged by bewildering instructions and addi- tional instructions from the industrious de- partment in question, and their lot, like that of the policeman in "The Pirates of Pen- zance," is not a happy one. It has been too hastily concluded by many required to fill up forms this week (I daresay most of them did it on Sunday) that the precise and detailed in- formation called for with reference to one's occupation is unprecedented, and totally un- warrantable assumptions have consequently been made. The last census was taken in 1911—only four years or so ago—and many who then filled up papers must have already forgotten that they were then required to indicate the exact kind of work they did, and the article or material worked at. Then, as now, it was not sufficient for one to describe himself as collector, agent, assistant, engi- neer, pensioner, or stoker;, details had in each instance to be supplied. The public memory is proverbially short, and individuals with axes to grind often presume on this more or leas successfully. • Vagaries of Lightning.

In the part of the country in which I am sojourning (for the reason given above, I know not how it may. be at Warrington) there has been a succession of daily thunderstorms without parallel in -my, experience. I take it for granted that all my readers are well aware that it is more or less dangerous—according to the nature of the tree—to shelter under tree during .such a visitation simply for the sake of keeping dry. Assuredly, it were better to get drenched than thus to provide an easy path for the passage to earth of a current of electricity. The strange phenome- non known as globe lightning still puzzles the scientists. I once witnessed it in unforget- tittle circumstances. I was visiting a relative, and was standing by a .window watching the lightning's freaks during a heavy storm, when on a building a short distance away there fell a huge ball of vivid blue flake which burst with disastrous results to the devoted pro- perty. It was a tremendous explosion com- parable only within my own knowledge with one occurring years afterwards at a farmhouse in another part of the country to which I had meanwhile removed. -In that case the fire- ball entered the, open door and drifted up the chimney, which was wrecked. The terrified -tediant had suffered from lightning previously, but not so seriously. Being a man of sub- stance, he had begun—to contemplate retire- ment into private life. The globe lightning hastened his departure. He still lives—in a Southport suburb, though', not on an' isolated farm. A Lever Story.

One day this week, as the rains descended and the floods came and beat upon my "digs," I cast about for something to read, and dis- covered a copy of a now defunct magazine con- taining a pretty story of Mr. (now Sir) W. H. Lever, showing how devoted he is to children and how in turn he is loved in no half-hearted way by the little folk of his model industrial village, Port Sunlight. They have an annual holiday outing, and on one such occasion had

Continued in Next column.

great fun on the merry-go-rounds at New Brighton. _ _

Mr. Lever was looking on at the sport, when two little girls came up to him and begged him to take them for a ride. .He assented, and thoroughly enjoyed the new experience of dipping up and down and swinging round on the mechanical horses. Said one little girl: "Mr. Lever, I've got a very rich aunt at New Brighton; she's got two hundred pounds saved up." "My word," said Mr. Lever, " she, is rich!" " But you've got more than that, Mr.' Lever,' said the other little girl. To which the .sOap king replied.: "Well, you know, I keep on spending money; and you can't have your money if you spend it, can you ?" This (proceeds the narrator) was a problem in economics which floored the little girls. At last one timidly inquired: ".But are you very poor now, Mr. Lover" " Well," Said he, "I keep spending my money, and what- ever shall Ido when it's all gone?" Like a flash came the answer from the two little girls in -unison " Come and live with us!" "Now, I have received a great many invitations in my lifetime," declares Mr. Lever, " but I ,never received one, nor ever can, so dear and sd sweet to me as that."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 21, 1915.

PARISH CLERKS kti* THEIR TRIALS FARM AND GARDEN.

The Coming Of The Bar-ei Organ.

RAISING YOUNG PIGS.

Those who attempt 'to raise a large number of young pigs with limited range and without

abundant green forage. of some; kind will be disappointed at the results. The large amount of range is beneficial, but .abundant •forage is the most important. The pig-breeder should therefore plan to have abundant pasturage of some kind well adapted for growing young pigs. Too many people have the erroneous impression that pigs do not require much, if anything; but grain or concentrated fopd, and that green fodder and bulky food are unnecessary. It may be true that pigs can get aTong N%ithout bulky food better than other farm stock, but there is really no variety of farm stock that more quickly shows the good effects of a liberal supply of forage of good quality: On tarins where it is not practicable to pasture a clover or lucerne layer, rape is an excellent substitute for reguminous plants. Young pigs up to 1001 b. weight can be very .satisfactorily developed by giving them the run of such grazing and supplementing it by a little grain food. A good substitute for skim milk is fine wheat middlings made into a rather thin slop with water. A good way to manage .young Pigs is to -give them a run out on grazing during the day, and a feed of soaked maize in the evening, and a feed of middlings in the morning. So managed, pigs will keep healthy and will pay as well as, or better than, any other farm - Stock.—T., in "Farm and Garden."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
COMEDY ilr\l CHURCH.

By ARTHUR

When the barrel organ was invented it was hailed as a blessing. Clergymen, who had suffered from the wrangling of clerks and choirs, saw in it a solution of every difficulty. No longer would there be orchestras or choirs to go on strike, and irritable clerks would no longer fling deadly scowls at the singers' gal- lery. It would only be a matter of putting the indicator to the right figure and letting go at the right moment. Others had suffered besides the clergy, and when subscription lists were opened the money flowed in. We can condone the hastiness of the clergy to embrace this new thing. Some of them had endured much, and endured it long. The Vicar of Buxted, for instance, when he buried his old clerk, wrote, in the register, "Whose melody warbled forth as if he had been thumped on the back with a stone." The provocation must have been great that stirred to such post-mortem revenge. But it was all a mistake. It would have been better to keep the village orchestra, to tame it, to surplice it, to do anything, in short, rather than substitute a machine. But neither clerks nor clergy had previous experi- ence to go upon, and the barrel organ came in with eulogies and hopes and a great welcome.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
BARREL ORGAN EPISODES.

The parish clerk took to the new instru- ment. It was a thing of which the common multitude stood in awe, and he, he alone, understood it. It 'pleased him that they Should be amazed that he could get so many tunes out of box, and he kept the secret of the changing of the barrels all to himself. The organ was usually kept behind curtains, and the clerk, after announcing the psalm, would disappear behind the screen and let off the tune. There would be a click and a faint groan, and then the old-100th would wail down the aisle. Of course little hitches occurred. At a church in Huntingdon a congregation waited very patiently for ilusic and heard only sun- dry noises of wood and metal. Then a troubled face appeared through the curtain and looked up at the vicar. It was the clerk. " Dang it. Ah con make nowt on her," he said: It had been discovered that barrel organs, like choirs, could go on strike. At Aston, in Yorkshire, the psalm came to a sudden stop. The clerk appeared after a long pause holding a piece of the organ handle and looking appbalingly at the pulpit. "Please sor," he said, "the handle's broke; what are we fort do?" The changing of the barrels was not always successfully accomplished, and there were various embarrassments, but these were small things to congregations which had grown up) under a choir and orchestra. Take the follow- ing as a picture of the days (sixty-five years ago) of the barrel organ. The place is Law- ton near Congleton, and the writer is Mr. John Smallwood. "The poor old clerk was often much embarrassed and exasperated by the old squire, Mr. C. B. Lawton, who was somewhat whimsical.in his ways. This gentle- man used to enter the church by his own private door' and go to his large, square, high-panelled family pew, and when the vicar gave out a hymn, he used often to shout out, "Here, hold on! I don't like that one; let's have hymn No. 25," or some such effort in psalmody. This request, or command, used to upset the organ arrangement, and the poor old clerk had to rummage among his barrels to get a suitable tune, and the operation, even if successful, took at least ten minutes, dur- ing which time a large amount of squeaking, and tile sounds of the writhing of woodwork and snapping of sundry catches were heard in the church. But the congregation was accus- tomed to the performance and thought little of it."

We are forgetting to say that there is a very good and lifelike presentation of the days when the bauel organ came, in Hewett's "Parish Clerk," a story of a Sussex vil- lage, its hero being Davy Diggs, the clerk. A vestry meeting was held to consider the buying of a " grindstun " organ (such was the rustic name for the thing that was worked like a grindstone), with half a dozen .simple psalm tunes in it, which Davy was to turn while the parson put on his gown in prepara- tion for the sermon. The clerk was ordered to write to the squire to ask for a liberal sub- scription. His appeal was as follows : "The Squir, Sur, - - . _

_ . "Me and farmer Field and the rest of the genelmen In vestri sembled Thinks the parson want parish Relif in shape of A Grind- stun orgin between Survisses--i am to grind him and the sundy skool kildren is to sing to him wile he Gos out of is sete. "We liv it to yurself wart to giv as we don't wont to _limit yur malevolens.

"Your Obedunt servunt, "Davy Diggs." The author of this letter was the only school- master the village could boast, and among other things he taught spelling. He was quite conscious, 'however, spelling was not his strong point, and laid great stress upon Scripture, because he knew that it was. He taught the Psalms and Catechism, and Was grieved when the vicar restrained him from teaching them the services for the Churching of Women and Burials, " 'cos he knowed um by heart himself and could teach urn like

===== 0002601_19150821_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CLERK AS POET.

Barrel organ music is apt to be jerky at the

best, and ;Cry much so when the handle is turned unsteadily. But irregularity was a trifling difficulty. It was only ,a matter of learning to breathe at the same speed as the handle, and get the emphasis with a similar jerk. In some hymns the special emphasis was an advantage, though it has to be ad- mitted that it was inconvenient in others. Sometimes when the clerk undertook to write a special hymn for a special occasion (as he sometimes did), he adapted his words with a fine sense of effect. Here is an example. It happened in Somersetshire. The Lord Bishop of the Diocese was to visit the church, and the clerk thought the occasion demanded a special hymn. The verses were then lined out two at a time and the clerk, after repeat- ing the Tines, turned to the handle of the

===== 0002601_19150821_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.-

continued.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNFIELD.

organ and ground out the music. The special hymn on this occasion was based on Tate and Brady's version of the 67th Psalm (" Why do ye skip ye high hills ?"), and the effusion and hymn were after this manner: "Let us zing to the Praise an' Glory of God part of the Zixty-zeventh Zalm;. zspeshul varshun zspech'ly 'dapted vur t' cashion : W'y 'op ye zo ye little 'ills? And what var du 'ee zskip ? Is it a'cause ter prach too we Is cum'd ma Lord Biship ? W'y zskip ye zo ye little 'ills? An' whot var du 'ee 'op? Is it a'cause to prach too we Is cum'd me Lord Bishop?

Then let us awl arize an' zing, An' let us awl stric uP, An' zing a glawrious zong uv praze; An' bless me Lord Bishup.

But not all the poetry of the barrel organ period was so bad. It was the Parish Clerk, of St. Mary's, Islington, Thomas Bill3y, who wrote "Here we suffer grief and pain," and that little jerky ditty lived for a long time, and was sung all round the world. Indeed, it is living yet.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ORGAN THAT WOULD NOT STOP.

It was generally felt that an advance had been made when an organ was invented which had a spring instead of a " grindstun " handle. The clerk released the catch at the right moment and needed to do no more until the catch was put in at the end of the psalm. But here again the church revealed fallibility of judgment. The spring organs behaved very well for a time—long enough to secure their adoption in many quarters. Then under the influence, of wear and perhaps perversity they showed signs of refusing to be amenable to reason. There are several stories of organs that would not stop until the spring had run down. In o,ne Lancashire church it , is said that the clerk, in despair, carried the wailing thing down the aisle and into the churchyard, where he sat upon it in the hope of crushing it into silence, and that even after this treatment, while the vicar preached it could be heard far off among the tombstones doing its hardest at the "Old Hundredth." THE DAY OF THE HARMONIUM.

In welcoming the barrel organ the Parish Clerk did not realise that he was sealing his own fate. He knew, perhaps, that he was abolishing the choir and orchestra, but that mattered little, seeing that it left him more than ever the musical autocrat.

Then came the day of the harmonium. This innocent little " kist of whistles " could be kept under control. It could play an inde- finite number of tunes ; it needed the buying of no fresh barrels to keep it up to date. And there was the vicar's daughter or the school mistress who could play it. The clerk realised by instinct that changes were imminent and hated the new-fangled music machine. The clerk at Swanscombe used to give out the hymn and with a scornful gesture bellow out, " Now then, strike up, miss." A few more years there was no need for the clerk. The vicar and organ could do it all. . THE CHURCH AS COBBLER'S RECEIV-

===== 0002601_19150821_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
ING HOUSE.

The mention of hawton and its squire re- minds us that there are other engaging aspects of the ancient parish clerk. Mr. Smallman, whom we have already quoted, wrote also some memories of the parish clerk. The period is 1850 to 1860. We quote again from his letter. "Lawton is a lovely spot, miles from any- where, and the congregation was formed of well-to-do farmers, who came from the scat- tered farmsteads. How well do I remember the old parish clerk and the numerous duties which fell to his lot! He united in his person the offices of clerk, sexton, beadle, church- keeper, organ-grinder and ringer. Old Clerk Briscall kept a small village shop about two miles from the church. He was also the vil- lage shoemaker. A curious system prevailed. As you entered the church, near the large stove, you would see a long bench, and under this bench a row of boots and shoes. If any one wanted his boots to be mended, he would take them to church with him and put them under the bench. These were carried home by the clerk in a sack and brought back on the following Sunday neatly soled and heeled. It would seem strange now if on entering a church our eyes should light upon a row of farmers' dirty old boots and the freshly- mended evidence of the clerk's skill."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EPITAPH VENDOR.

The clerk as village cobbler or grocer was common. He was often also an epitaph specialist. To inscribe a gravestone without a " werse " was an unthinkable omission in the good old days, and as people did not like to copy others in the matter, they valued an original suggestion. It was to meet this felt need that the clerk of one of the Salisbury Churches issued the following advertisement: "John Hopkins, parish clerk and under- taker, sells epitaphs of all sorts and prices. Shaves neat, and plays the basoon. Teeth drawn' and the Salisbury journal read gratis every Sunday morning at eight. A school for psalmody every _Thursday evening, when

thy eon .born- blind will play the fiddle.— Speci.-

men epitaph on my wife:

My wife ten years, not much to my ease, But now she is dead,lin caelo quies. Great variety can be seen within.—Your humble servant, John Hopkins." A Yorkshire clerk was innkeeper and thought it appropriate, to call his house "Heaven's Gate." At a funeral when the clergyman had --concluded the §-ervice he in- variably stood up and said, "Friends of the corpse is respectfully informed that they can get the best of refreshments at my house zleross the way." It is hard to round up an article like this with not a third of our stories told, but the mention of epitaphs gives us our opportunity. The most admirable inscription ever placed over a clerk is in Crayford Churchyard: The life of this clerk was just threescore and

ten' Nearly half of which time he had sung out

Amen. In his youth he had married like other young

men, But his wife died one day—so he chanted

Amen. A second he took—she departed—what then? He married and buried a third with Amen. Thus his joys and his sorrows were treble, but then His voice was deep base, as he sung out Amen. On the horn he could blow as well as most

So his born was exalted to blowing Amen. But he lost all his wind after three score and

ten, • And here with three wives he waits till again The trumpet shall rouse him to sing out Amen. The mention of a succession of wives sug- gests another " paiish clerk " story. In a certain graveyard the, clerk would point visit- ors to a tomb and say: "This 'ere is the grave of Thomas 'Ooper and his eleven wives."

"Eleven wives. What—" the lady visitors would gasp. Whereupon the old man would look very grave and say, " Well, mum, ,yer see, it was a kind of 'obby of his'n."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
MARKETING CHEESE.

In the July "Journal "of theADepartment of Agriculture and• Technical Instruction for Ireland, Mr. A. Poole Wilson says:— As regards marketing cheese the following notes may be instructive to English readers, as well as Fish, who may be thinking of making cheese for general consumption:— The chief market for cheese being in Great Britain, owing to the transhipment at the:ports of departure and arrival, it is necessary to pack all cheese in strong Crates. To this extent the Irish producer is handicapped. The. British maker, in the majority of cases, places cheese unpacked on clean straw in the bottom of a cart or railway truck. • In Great Britain,cheese is sold as follows:- 1. By the maker's at the various cheese fairs or markets which may be held weekly, as at Highbridge, in Somerset (Caerphilly and Cheddar); once or twice a month, as at Lan- caster and ,Preston .(Lancashire, cheese); •Nant-. wich, Whitchurch,' and Chester (Cheshire cheese). These fairs are attended by brokers buying for themselves or by the buyers of wholesale houses. The brokers may sell again to wholesale merchants. 2. The wholesale merchants may send their buyers to the;dairies or factories at certain periods to examine the cheese and make offers for the stock in hand. 3. The cheese may be consigned to wholesale houses who will sell it on commission. 4. The cheese may be sold at firm prices. The fourth method depends on the manager having a good knowledge of the special requirements of the customer and a good idea as to quality of cheese. This method is, however, slowly grow- ing. A number of buyers, such as 'grocers and wholesale merchants, prefer to contract for the delivery of definite quantities weekly at a fixed price for three, six, nine or twelve months. This is a method of selling which requires great judgment and consideration. The quality of the cheese must be good and uniform or it may happen that if prices drop the buyer becomes more critical of quality, while if prices rise the buyer is satisfied unless quality varies too much.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

RAMBLER ROSES.

In most gardens one at least of these finds a place, and if plenty of bloom is looked for ne*t year all old and exhausted growths, now that the flowering is over, should be at once removed. The young rods that will, if well ripened, pro- duce the finest trusses of bloom next year are now pushing strongly from the base of the plants, and these should be encouraged in every way. In the case of two or three year bid plants, some of the old wood may be retained, but the old plants are benefited by having all such Cut out.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
GATHERING VEGETABLES.

A common error is to wait until the bulk of the crop is 'well advanced. Not only are most vegetableS at their best before they reach full size, as, for example, cauliflowers and runner beans, but if the ,cutting of the former is too long delayed the head takes on a gre!rn appear- ance, and becomes open and loose, the result being that when cooked the curd is-bitter and wanting in that pleasant flavour which be- tokens a quickly-grown and time-harvested head. Again, how often do we find runner beans allowed to remain on the plants till the seeds are quite prominent and the flesh tough and hard. The result, in 'this case, is that the later blooms fail to form, and if they do form, the flowers fail to set, the consequent loss being very great.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VALUE OF SOOT WATER.

This is not only beneficial when applied to the roots of growing crops, but syringed in a clear state over the foliage of such plants as chrysan- themums it checks the attacks of insects and gives to the leaves that bright green look that betokens healthy growth. A well-known gar- dener of our acquaintance used to dust the leaves of his chrysanthemums with old soot, and we have never seen such healthy foliage as his plants carried: To have the soot water clear, the best way is to put it into a coarse bag and immerse it in "a tub of water, frequently mov- ing the bag and allowing it to remain in the, tub until the water is deeply stained, when the bag may be taken out, the soot emptied' out, and the bag dried ready for use again. In many gardens the celery: fly is very destructive,' and to prevent its inroads we have found nothing so good as frequently dusting the plants with soot. This, however, should always be begun before there are any signs of the leaf miner, which if not destroyed will ruin the crop.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
DAHLIAS.

These are now coming into bloom, and as, owing to the heavy rains we have had, they are growing very strongly, constant attention is necessary in the way of tying the shoots to prevent their being broken off. Earwigs, we find, are also very troublesome. These must be trapped by putting some dry moss into small pots, fixing these on the tops of the stakes: Hollow beanstalks are also useful traps. Exam- ine the pots and stalks every morning, and if any earwigs are found, shake them into a pail of water on which some paraffin is floating.— "Wick," in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY AND EGGS.

How Most People Can Help The Food

Supply.

_ . Every additional egg and every extra chicken reared will help the food supply, if produced under proper conditions, and there is at the present time special need to increase the num- bers of both eggs and poultry. Any reduction in the laying or breeding stock of poultry kept is against the best interests of both the indi- vidual and the nation, and each society should do all it can to extend poultry-keeping in its dis- trict—again, either by individuals or collectively by co-operation. The number of Persons who could readily keep poultry is very great, and a few birds • kept by each would not only enable them ,to utilise much household and garden "waste," but to utilise it in the economic pro- Auction of eggs and table birds and add to the resources of the country. The Board of Agri- culture have issued. a number of leaflets' on poultry. In some districts where ;there are fairly large areas available, such as commons, it may be possible to take up:, the co-operative rearing of turkeys, while ducks can even more commonly be bred and fattened. • While it may often be desirable and profitable to sell the eggs produced when new-laid, the societies may usefully Consider whether, their members cannot act in concert, purchase jars and materials in bulk, and Preserve eggs for future use. By this "means it may be possible for a small district or village to put by some thousands of eggs during the " cheap " season for use when egg's are dear, and so save the meat bill.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVED CROPS.

Fine Weather __Needed To Reap Good

Harvests.

" Torrential " rains, " terrific " hailstorms, and levelling .winds such. as ,many of our ,corre- spondents.speak of, says the "Times," reporting on the state of the crops, are extremely injurious to 'grain crops, then at their tallest as to straw and their heaviest as to ear. The consequence is that our August report is full of complaints from all the great English corn-growing coun- ties:

It is difficult to localise these destructive tornadoes, but they seem to have Visited a large number of English counties. Thus we tear of violent storms in Cambridgeshire; all crops knocked about in Essex; "awful" storms in Lincolnshire; floods, and crops washed away in Suffolk; crops mauled and lodged in East York- shire; all cereals and hops damaged by gales and lieavy rain in Kent; and " terrific " storms in Surrey. In the West we are informed that 50 per cent. of all corn crops were ruined over large tracts in Gloucester and Hereford, while Shropshire records ,6in. of rain during the month.

In spite of these drawbacks the general report is an improvement upon that for July, when it general persistent drought was the principal trodble. Still, the condition of all the principal crops except potatoes is distinctly below the ,average of the previous 10 years, and as the zenith has been reached we can scarcely hope to see higher figures later. In the North, wheat is reported to be short in the straw and thin upon the ground, but well "eared:" 4' In many places its -further develop- ment will be checked by rust and blight, which always attack it in dull and rainy, weather— especially in late summer. Barley shows a- marked improvement since July Ist. Suffolk and Surrey in fact stand out prominently among the less favourable esti- mates of all the three cereals. Still, in spite of a w_neral recorded improvement, the barley crop is certain to prove disappointing on account of the poor reports from the principal producing enun ties.

Oats, as might be expected in such a season, show the greatest improvement among cereal crops. They are 7 points above the July esti- mate and only, 2 below the 10 years' average. They can ny longer, be described as one of the worst crops on record, and bid fair to improve still farther. They are also very widely grown in the later districts and are still green and 'growing.

- Beans are by common consent considered to be ,a partial failure on account of the collapse of the spring-sown portion of the crop. Potatoes and root crops appear to have bene- fited in a remarkable manner by the change from drought to moisture.. The improvement is very marked in almost all cases, 'and amounts to 10 points in many and 20 in some. Potatoes have been lifted out of the ruck and now rank above the average for the preceding 10 years. In July they worked out 5 points below. The chief danger now lies in a general attack of disease, which the present weather conditions is cettain to encourage.

Turnips and Swedes have sprung suddenly into existence. In July we were unable to construct averages; but thanks to abundance of rain we are now likely to secure excellent crops of both.

Pastures are now full of grass, but hay has suffered in most localities.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVEMENT IN LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE.

In Cheshire on July 1 the wheat crop pro- mised 100 per cent. of perfection, and on August Ist the figure remained at 100. Barley advanced from 75 to 81; and oats from 66 to 85. For beans there are no returns for the county. Potatoes improved from 77 in July to 86 in August; roots., (except mangels), for which there were no returns in July, now stand at 81. In Lancashire the standard of the wheat crop advanced between July 1 and August 1 from 90 to 98.3; barley from 83 to 91.5; and oats from 78 to 88.3.

For beans there are no returns, but potatoes advanced, from 84 to 95; and roots (except mangels) from 73 to 95.

CHESHIRE CORN HARVEST.

Farmers in East Cheshire began corn-cutting during last week-end. Rain interfered with the operations, and both' wheat and oats were badly laid, hundreds of acres being affected. The area of wheat in Cheshire is said to be much larger than last year. •

===== 0002601_19150821_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
"NEV*" LAID" AND " FRESH "

EGGS.

The President of the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries has under consideration the steps Which can be taken, in the interests of the home producers and of sellers of genuine new-laid apd fresh eggs, to deal more effectively w.ith the application of the descriptions "new laid" and " fresh " to eggs which, in view of their age or condition, should not be described by these terms, and he has directed an inquiry to be made into the meaning of the terms "new laid" and " fresh "as applies to eggs. The principal ques- tion involved is the number of days from the date of laying up to which an egg may properly be described by these terms. A circular has been addressed to secretaries of societies and others, connected with the pro- duction and distribution of eggs, and specific questions are put as under:— •

(1) Up to wliat number of days from the date of laying May an egg he properly described as "new laid"?

(2) Up to what number of days from the date of laylitg may an egg be properly described as "fresh'?

(3) Assuming that an egg has not been laid for more than the periods indicated in replies to questions 1 and 2, but is in bad condition, or has been taken from an incubator or nest, is there a false description if it is described as "'new laid" or "fresh," even if it is sterile?

The President of the Board expresses the opinion that "the descriptions 'new laid' and ' fresh ' are not properly applicable to eggs pro- duced in the United Kingdom or imported from foreign countries which have been preserved or Cold stored."

THE YORKSHIRE DALES.

(Queries and correspondence should be addressed to "The Hub," c/o The Editor. Replies can- not be sent by post and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

A delightful cycling or motoring trip is that through: the Yorkshire Dales. Every kind of scenery is to be met with from the quiet country lane to ,the black and rugged moor. In places the hills are severe, but generally speak- ing the road surfaces are excellent. Starting from Leeds the Otley road should be taken; thence by the Wharfe to Ilkley, Bolton Bridge and the roaring Strid, -then over the moors to Plateley, Bridge and Ripon. Four miles from Ripon is Fountains Abbey, which should cer- tainly be visited, while Ripon Cathedral itself is very fine indeed. The road now proceeds almost due north for Masham (1 mile from which is Jervaulx Abbey) and Wensley: then up lovely Wensleydale by Aysgarth to Hawes. A rough journey over the.Buttertubs Pass from Hawes brings one to Huker, at the head of Swaledale. The ride down this valley is ex- ceptionally fine, the river Swale being followed all the way to Richmond, a charming old-world town with some very fine views. Thence the road to Gilling and Rokeby should be taken where the meeting of Tees and Greta has in- spired Scott and Turner and many a poet and painter. From Rokeby the road should be retraced for a couple of miles, where the left- hand turning should be taken through the rich woods for Ovington, Winston, Gainford, Pierce- bridge and Darlington. The distance of the route as described above is 136 f Miles. and it affords an opportunity of seeing the finest of the Yorkshire Dales. There are other delight- ful dales which may be visited, but the route I have described includes perhaps the best of the scenery, while Leeds and Darlington, both easily accessible bt rail, make good starting and finishing points.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEANING THE CHAIN.

As I pointed out two or three weeks ago, it is quite possible to keep the chain in good run- ning order by cleaning it when it is on the bicycle. There are times, however, when it is better to give it a thorough clean, and this can only be accomplished by taking it off the Machine. One of the best methods is as fol- lows :—When the chain nut has been unscrewed, the chain should be brushed hard and then soaked for a .few hours in 'paraffin oil. Then it should be rinsed in a fresh supply of Paraffin. After being hung up to dry, it should he coiled up and /placed in a suitable vessel and covered with sperm oil and left overnight. The sperm oil will penetrate to all the work- ing parts. The surplus oil is then drained off. the chain rubbed dry with a cloth and finally robbed with a mixture of methylated spirit and black lead. The Tame mixture should be used for the cog wheels. Such cleaning will make the job thoroughly satisfactory.

THE FAULTS OF PICNICKERS.

- This subject was brought to my noti9e on the day after Bank Holiday when visiting a well- known "beauty spot,-". which by the way is situated in private grounds. Permission to visitors is accorded by the owner on certain days of the year, August 2nd being one of them. I was shown over the park the following day, and I .was shocked to see the vast quantities of paper bags, bottles, &c., that had been thrown about. I am afraid this is. not an isolated in- stance, since I have seen the same elsewhere on other occasions. Cyclists are, as a rule, very careful in the disposal of their refuse, but this does not apply to all. We should do all we can by behaving prpperly, to encourage owners to make public their properties. But the above is hardly the way in which to do it. If you lent yoUr own little garden to strangers. how would you like them to leave it covered with paper and other refuse?

BENZOLE FOR MOTOR CYCLISTS.

A large number of motor cyclists would be using bnzole as a fuel, if they realised that the expense of spirit can be cut down in this way, and moreover, that benzole has no deleterious effect on the engine. Many may doubt the truth of the latter statement, but all the same it is a fact. I have tried it myself, and now I use it in place of petrol. Extensive tests have been carried out with this liquid and they have all proved its suitability. For, two- stroke and four-stroke engines it is equally suc- cessful, and the mileage per gallon can be in- creased, particularly in the case of the former. Only a slight alteration or adjustment to the

PECIA V• 1•1110. 4

carburetter is necessary. Apart from this there is nothing to prevent anyone from saving mond by using benzoic' in place of petrol.

Very few motor cyclists Ili" fail to appreciate the importance of this subject.'

WOMEN ASYLUM NURSES.

Protest Against Employment In Male

Wards.

The National Asylum Workers 'Union have iSsued a strongly-worded manifesto on the phy- sical and moral danger of employing female nurses in male asylum wards.

"We view with serious concern," states the Executive Council, "the, adoption by many asylum authorities of a policy whichl has for its object the partial substitution of female nurses for male attendants in the care and charge of insane male patients. , We desire to record our unanimous • condemnation of a prac- tice which will prove physically and morally detrimental to the nurses and injurious ,to the patients.

"The work is of a nature entirely unfitted for women to perform, involving degrading 'duties, repellant to all the finer instincts of womanhood, and accompanied by a danger understandable .only rby those actively associ- ated with asylum work. To the point of view of the patients' welfare, we deprecate the bringing of , male patients, often morally per- verted, into close association with persons gf the opposite sex."

POINTS OF WIRING.

To obtain the best results from a lighting installation there should be no joints in the' wiring. This is a point which is frequentli overlooked by motorists. As far as posible each length of cable from terminal to terminai should 'be complete. There are times whe° this cannot be done. In such eases a pro Per electrical joint should be made. It is necessorY to solder the joint, but instead of using t.he 'ordinary flux, namely, killed spirit or chlorlde. of zinc, resin should be employed. The formo is not nearly so satisfactory, though it is true that it iS easier to use, and the work can b• done more rapidly. Special insulated tar* should be used to cover up the joint for two reasons One is to prevent moisture from PePe- trating, and the other to hinder 'a contact being made. The former point is one that is fre- quently forgotten, but it is very important.

CURATE'S CAPTURE.

Arrested "Actor" Attempts To Jump Bridge Parapet.

BEGGING LETTERS.

Some excitement was created on Warrington Bridge 'about three o'clock on Wednesday after- noon, by an attempt on the part of a man who had just esdaped from a detective to jump the parapet. The detective, however, was too quick tor him and succeeded in preventing his prisoner from carrying out his purpose. A -considerable crowd gathered. The police officer obtained the assistance of a colleague (P.C. Harrison) before resuming progress to the Police Station. " Will You Walk into My Parlour?"

The first part of ' this little drama was' enacted at .356, Wilderspool-road, the residence of the Rev. Charles F. Waller, curate of St. James' Church, Latchford. Somewhere about two o'clock, the domestic employed at the house answered the door to the man, who subsequently caused the excitement on the Bridge. - He handed her a letter addressed to the Rev. C. F. Waller. The maid took the letter in and the addressee found that it was an appeal for assistance, the writer stating that he was an actor who had been taken from Portsmouth to Preston by a wrong .manager and there robbed.

Having read the letter, the clergyman gave instruction for the writer to be Shown into the parlour. Here the visitor showed his host,the bad condition of his boots. Asked if he wanted 'Money to have them mended, he replied that if he was given money he *mid pay it back little by little. The Rev. C. F. Waller then asked this caller if he could wait twenty minutes, and, receiving an answer in the affirmative, went to see the Vicar of Stockton Heath (who lives only a short distance away) for advice. This gentle- man recommended that the Police be sent for. and his brother minister carried the recom- mendation into effect.

Four Letters And Two Names. When Detective Turner arrived, he read till) letter and,, ,on searching the writer, found three similar letters, with slight alterations of names of places; and also several addresses of local ministers. , 'rwp of the letters were signed; Bernard Wilson, and the other•two (in- cluding that sent in to the Rev. C. P. Waller): Alexander Edwards.

Under the latter • name, the prisoner was charged, at the Borough Court, on Thursday morning, before M. J, Rymer Young (in the chair), .Mr. A. J. Jolley, Dr. Naden and Mr. W. T. Williams, with begging. The Superintendent outlined the case and asked for a remand, to enable the police to make inquiries as to prisoner's identity. " I Thought He Might Show Sympathy."

Asked if he had ,anything to say, prisoner (a young man of superior appearance and a cultured accent) said: "I was on my way to Portsmouth. I didn't aSk the clergyman for any money. When he said would a sovereign cover my expenses, I refused the money and said I could not accept it, as I didn't know when I could pay it back. Then he said could I. wait twenty minutes; and I said I could, and waited. I thought he might sho* me sympathy in the way I needed. He admits that I never asked him for a farthing." The Superintendent stated that, when .arrested, prisoner had a war service button in his'coat.. He'said he found it. Prisoner was remanded for a week.

BOY'S PERILOUS CLIMB ON GREAT ORME.

Exciting Rescue.

dn Monday forenoon considerable excitement was caused at Llandudno by the rescue of a boy of fourteen or fifteen from the cliff of the Great Orme's Head overlooking the town. The youth, whose name is Howard Ben Hale, and whose home is at Bootle, was spending a holi- day at Llandudno, and he went climbing on the precipitous face of the height known as pen Dinas. It is possible to creep along very narrow grass-covered •ledges, below• which is- a .Sheer drop of forty pr fifty feet into a disused quarry. Hale slipped, but managed to hang on to a clump of grass, his legs overhanging the ledge. From this position it was impossible for him to escape without help, and in time, if . that were not forthcoming, lie would have fallen from exhaustion and been Fortunately, an elderly man, a labourer 'named Thomas Jones, of Tudno-street, hap- pened to notice the lad's perilous position. He obtained a rope ancWiurried to his assistance, it being a' question,. Nfriether • lie could- reach the ledge before the:boy's strength gave way. Jones won the race, and,, with the assistance of Private G. Loe, of the' 15th tLondon Welsh) Battalion of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, wilo, ! followed him to. the ledge,' Hale was 'Pulled nV and allowed to rest -on the ledge. He was InuCh exhausted, :and it took half an hour for him to recover sufficiently to be lowered by means of the rope to a ledge lower down, where Privates J. Oliver and A. W. Gee, of the London Welsh, and Mr. A. R. Hughes, a local resident, were waiting to receive him. _ _

The work- of rescue could be.: Seen from Alostyn-street and Other parts of the toWn, and, as the news quickly spread that someone was in difficulties on The cliff, the operations of the rescuers were 'watched by crowds of people.

TROOPS CHEER LLOYD GEORGE SHELLS.

Scene After the Battle of Hoage.

"

An officer of the Durhara, who took part in the battle of Hooge, has written to a friend at Ifitchin, describing the final charge, and adds: It was the new Lloyd George shells which gave us the heart to make the charge, after being so heavily hit.. These new shells are magnificent, and after Our fellows got into the captured trenches they gave three cheers for Lloyd George.

Ito close their London branch. There has been no demand for Court dresses, race gowns, or evening frocks, and on the top of this there is the league of- ladies moving in the highest circles in the land, who advocate a pledge to wear only "clothes." • That was ' the last straw, and it has caused the closing of Messrs. Worth, who have been established in London nearly twenty years, and employ 200 people. As all the leading costumiers are reducing their staffs, it will be difficult for the employes to find fresh situations, and naturally they do not see that the Women's War Economy League serves any useful purpose. The political economist says it is so much waste that twelve women should be employed for a fortnight on a dress for a duchess, who will wear it only for a few hours. No doubt he is right, but those dressmakers will say they have to take the world as they find it, and they might ask what is the duchess going to 'do with the money she saves, by dispensing with the dress? Whe family income is derived from property, arid although the Government will want more of it, the hoarding of rents simply- means the accumulation of wealth in the hands of the few. It will take some time to adjust old established , social conditions to the new regime of •war

economies.

Tunics Still In Fashion.

The tunic has by no means disappeared from the scheme of dresS, for it is too useful to be lightly discarded. It was not at its test last year, worn over'.the hobbles skirts -that' then prevailed. this summer it is far more effective for it requires width and fullness undeineath, and also length to bring out its graceful lines. The tunic dresses designed for this year's smart river wear, are particularly charming, in embroidered voile. The long overdress reaches almost to the hem of the full underskirt, which is several inches from the ground. The long lines from the waist to almost the hem—since they run downwards—lend a grace to the figure, and a slimness to the silhouette that is always pleasing. The newest taffetas frocks for special occasions are made with overdressea of the lightest and filmiest of transparencies, which soften the brilliance of the silk. The Blouse Pre-eminent. The blouse continues to maintain a position of supreme importance, for there is no hour of the day that in one form or another it can- not be appropriately worn. Lingerie blouses are obviously to the fore in Au..st, and the shops abound in the most delightful designs in fine linen, lawn, and organdi muslin, Hand

south, where there were 300 boys besides a big staff of masters and servants to be catered for. They were very well fed, no lack of meat, and there were kitchen gardens in the grounds which supplied vegetables of all kinds. Yet they only got soup once, or at the most twice, a week, and always the same, which was a nondescript kind. At the school to Which ,my daughter _goes near London, and where there are in all from SO to 100 to be fee, the experience is exactly the same. What waste, when one considers the amount of meat, which includes so much bone, that must be used in such places. Is to the economy of soup there is also no doubt, as it certainly reduces the meat bill, besides using up scraps of all kinds. Many delightful vege- table soups can be made without meat or bones if half, milk is used instead of all water, and the addition to those of a pennyworth of cream is no extravagance, though it ,is extraordinary how shocked many people are at the idea of buying cream to put in soup. It makes all the difference to such so'ups as potato, vegetable marrolV, tomato and cauliflower, also artichoke and cream of barley, all of which can, if need be, be made without stock. And the boilings of a sheep's head make ex- cellent Scotch broth with barley and all kinds of vegetables cut small, and in summer the addition of green peas or beans is a great im- provement.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Women Volunteers At Vicker's.

The training of leisured and educated women in the making of munitions of war be- gan in earnest on the 9th July last, when the first volunteer party entered upon a three weeks' course at Vicker's works. Their object was to take the place of the regular factory hands on Sundays, and to undertake all over- time work, which the women employes could do only at the cost of a breakdown in health. Thus the output of munitions will be kept up, and the hands have the needful .rest. A hostel has been established, and here the volunteers are living, their earnings ranging from fifteen to nineteen shillings per week. The London County Council, with the authority of the Ministry of Munitions, together with the Board' of Education, is arranging six weeks' courses for the training of women in this work. There will be an examination at the end of the lessons, and a small fee charged for instruction. The Last Straw.

We are ,told that fresh war taxation is im- pending, but housewives declare that this will be intolerable if the Government cannot take some effective action to limit the prices of food and necessaries. Retailers have been giving another turn of the screw in hoisting prices, and grocers are now charging 40. per lb. for lump sugar, for which the wholesale price is but a fraction over 30. per lb. According to one of the largest firms of refiners, there is no scarcity or any change of recent date to justify a higher charge to the public for sugar. Pro- visions were dear enough before the war, but in many• towns prices as fixed by the local tradesmen, average a 40 per cent, increase, whilst the rise in wholesale prices has been nothing like so great. To families who are making no money out of the war, and whose income is Mainly spent on the necessaries of life, it means a war tax of 7s. or Bs. in the pound, and for the classes who form the bulk of the population, there is no margin left f9r further taxation on sugar, tea or other indis- pensable commodities.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable IteeipeS and Useful Suggestions.

Milk will quench a fire caused by an explod- ing lamp, water only spreading the oil.

Hiccough.

What. will stop hiccoughs? If for a child .a small lump of sugar with a drop of vinegar on it, and let the Child stick it. If a grown per- son small pieces .of ice applied suddenly, so as to surprise the patient, will usually stop hiccoughs, or drinks of hot coffee. Milk taken frequently is said to have the desired effect.

Offensive Feet., The following is a remedy for offensive feet. The feet should be washed daily in cold water and afterward rubbed thoroughly dry;- the water may contain ioz. powdered alum to the quart. Before putting. on the stockings, -which should be of thin flannel, powder the feet well, and especially between the toes, with potato starch.

Barley Scones. Take lib. of barley meal, put it in a basin with pne teaspoonful of salt, one small tea- spoonful of caster sugar, and one teaspoonful of bicarbonate of soda. Mix all well together. then stir in with a wooden spoon sufficient buttermilk to form a stiff dough. Roll out on the baking-board, cut into neatly shaped scones, and fire on a good hot girdle, turning the scones very carefully when the underside is cooked. Sir Walter Scott, in the "Fair Maid of Perth." referred to such scones as these when he said "barley scones to whiten your teeth.'

Highland Barley Scones. This is a very old retipe, but no more delicious novelty can be placed upon a breakfast table to-day. They must be freshly made from really good barley meal. Put 11b. of barley meal in a basin with one teaspoonful of salt, and mix these together. Stir in quickly sufficient boil- ing water to make a stiff dough. Knead very quickly, and. form into an oblong roll. Cut off a small piece, roll in a. little meal on the baking- board, and flatten out to. a very thin scone. Place on a hot girdle, and proceed to form the rest of the dough into scones in the same way. The scones must be fired on both sides as quickly as possible. .Failing a griddle, an oven shelf makes a good substitute, and maybe used equally well over a closed liage or a as cooker.—Muriel Sutherland, in "The Queen."

HERE. AND THERE.

Men are apt to worship what they cannot understand—women, for instance.

A woman always feels sorry for a man who has trouble with his wife, unless she happens to be his wife.

Women Farmers in Japan. A woman working on a ,farm in Japan. re- ceives from ten to twelve cents a day if she is boarding herself. If she lives in the family of her employer she is given from six to .seven .cent ft day. A man does not 'receive much More for. this kind of labour. In conformance with the general 'standard of living this wage is not low. Men Wear Cambs.

Ceylon is perhaps the only country in the world where men wear combs. It is the delight of the native males of the low country to wear their hair twisted into a coil at the back of the head, with .a horseshoe shaped tortoiseshell comb at the top. The women, curiously enough, do not affect this ornament., It appears that one of the great ambitions of the Cingalese man of humble position is to possess and wear a huge comb of the finest lustre and most perfect manufacture, and many indicate their higher social status by means of an additional comb, rising to a considerable height above their glossy coil.

'MID HELSBY HILLS.

Now, of course, children, there are a good many \more very fine entries, and I must com- mend 386 Niece May Tabernacle (Beamont C. School), for the following beautifully written letter :

"68, Marsh House-lane, Warrington, August 15th, 1915. "Dear Uncle Ned,—The holiday which I am describing is about a week spent at Helsby. It was delightful roaming over the hills and climbing to the top, looking for miles around me. • I could see the Mersey winding in and out, with its numerous boats. In the distance I could see the Overton Hills and Welsh mountains boldly standing out against the sky. A sunset could not be more beautiful than at Helsby, the sky being lit up with a red glare like a fire. It was the best holiday that I have ever .had.---Your loving niece, MAY TABERNACLE."

Capital, May, capital! (Shall I whisper in. your ear? You only just missed the prize this week, and I want you to persevere!) -

"2, Godfrey-street, Warrington. "Dear Uncle Ned,—l see you are asking your nephews to write about 'A Happy Holi- day.' I spent a very happy holiday in the country at Frodsham. I went on the swing- boats at Mersey View, and was also picking blackberries and crab apples. I climbed up the hill three times and gathered heather also. After that I had my tea in a big room with other children.—From your loving Nephew, HAROLD JONES." PADDLING AT WORMIT.

Ambroidery and stitchery is a feat on.; r 4 these interesting and fascinating garments, as is the new setting of the Raglan sleeve, which sometimes narrow off to a fine point at the neck. The square yoke too is seen again in the new skirts of warmer materials for the autumn. The blouse coat which, when it first appeared was named after the popular game of bridge, has taken on a new lease of life, and has evidently come to stay. Its merits arewmanifold, and it is as useful as it is attractive. Evening blouses are ma-de of the most fragile fabrics, and give the idea of fairy garments. There is, as a rule, a link between them, and the skirts with which they are worn; the material forming the jupe being used very sparingly for some part of the corsage, where the colour of the skirt is repeated.

Short Rest Gowns. Another garment that renews its youth and adds to-its beauty every season is the tea or rest gown, which has become indispensable to women who work, as well as to their leisured sisters. Utility, since the war changed everything, is the first consideration Where clothes are 'con- cerned, and the usefulness, as well as the com- fort of the rest gown are self-evident. Since this garment dispensed with its train, it has become much more popular, and it has gained by the change in more' ways than One. It is more youthful looking than formerly, consequently it appears to younger womerk. For evening wear and for informal dinners, however, the train is in evidence, and especially is 'thig the cab when the wearer is an elderly 'woman. The coatee of velvet, hanging from the shoulders by bretettes -4 beads, is a feature of this season's gowns. Sleeves, when of the bell shape, are rather short, but there is as Much variety in these as in the tea gowns themselves. Flies and Lawn Mowings.

To keep the housefly out of rooms occupied by children—also from contaminating their food —ig a Matter of the first importance. Flies' object to draughts and to the smell of strong chemicals, therefore windows should be widely opened• and a few drops of carbolic acid or creosote in a little water, if placed in each room, will .keep away the pests. The mowings from lawns constitute a real danger to health if left, as is usually the case, to rot in a heap in some ^orner of the garden. There they generate a erce but transient heat, and form an ideal nest for the egg of the housefly, which always chooses heat-producing substances in which to deposit its ova. The mowings should be burned, unless they can be given to horses, pigs, or fowls.

HINTS ON HEILTII.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Do not eat large quantities of protein food. The body can only use advantageously a modeuate amount for . building and repair material. The waste products that result from it are virulent poisons and pave the way for Bright's disease, high blood pressure, nervous prostration, and many other disoraers: Mpdern medical science has learned how to conquer practically every disease except those that result from overeating. Someone has said if you chew long you will live long. Soule people seem to think they do not have time to eat. They merely chop their food up enough so that they can swallow' it without choking. If we insist that the stomach shall do the work that was intended for the teeth, we are laying the foundatior for gastric ulcer, hyperacidity, and many other unpleasant disorders. We owe a debt of gratitude to Horace Fletcher for helping us to discover the importance of chewing.—David Paulson, in "Good Health."

English Extravagance. The over-eating and excessive number of meals in many English homes is spoken of in a letter from a French lady to the " Queen ": "You all say you are so 'hard up,' but why do you waste so much money and keep so many ser- vants? Your servants are kept at work all day long. Cups of tea are taken up to the bedrooms; this means time to cut bread and butter, and afterwards wash cups, teapots, &e. Then the men have to lay an elaborate breakfast-table, while the cook has to cook hot bread, hot dishes, &c. Instead of all this we have coffee and rolls put on the dining-room table, where everyone, ,excepting invalids, help, themselves, and there is no cooking except making the coffee. No sooner is your elaborate breakfast cleared away than the preparations for luncheon begin. Here, our dejenner and your luncheon are much the same,,but we don't then require more.hot cakes, sandwiches, &c., at fiv4. Your five o'clock must occupy the servants for nearly two hours, pre- paring and washing up. Then comes dinner. When do your men-servants find time to clean silver, lamps, &c.? No wonder you want three to our one. Then your lamps (this is in the country). I counted five lamps in the drawing- room last night. At home my father has one small reading lamp beside him, and my mother sits at a big round table, on which is one good lamp: My wife, myself, and the children all sit round, and one lamp serves for all." This man's wife opened her eyes, too, over the quantities of jam we consumed in summer. "Of course, I know sugar is cheap in England, but why give jam when there is plenty of fruit to be had. And biscuits. You English live on biscuits, I suppose, because your bread iA rather solid !"—Y.

(Arpley-street School), who writes to tell me:— "42, Warburton-street, Stockton Heath. "Dear Uncle Ned,—l spent a very happy holiday this year at Wormit, near Dundee. One day we went down to the beach and paddled, as it was a hot day. The river we paddled in was the • Tay. We had some fun in the garden when we reached 'home,' and when it was raining we went up in the attic to play, where we had some fun, and when we were tired playing we sat at the window watching the trains. As you don't like long composi- tions I will stop.—Your affectionate niece, DOROTHY BENNETT." Bravo, Dorothy F I am glad to hear that you' did not sulk because it was raining, but made the best of it! I know some boys and girls who cry when it rains, and won't do anything but make themselves a nuisance. Would you be- lieve ,it? But then, none of 'Uncle Ned's boys and girls are like that! I received your letter last week all right, Dorothy, only it came just too late to be judged—l am sorry to say.. How- ever, it is a good essay, so I will print it this week:— " Dear Uncle Ned,—l .am going to describe my train journey, coming back from Scotland, where_ I have been spending my holiday. The place I was staying at most of the time was called Abernethy. We stepped into the train and away it went. The next station I must mention; the name of it is the Bridge of Erne, where the famous regiment is, encamped there. Arriving tit Perth, we thought we would have a walk round as we had plenty of time. We saw several wounded soldiers with a Red Cioss nurse. Shortly we came back to the station, where we saw several soldiers and sailors, and a man with a big St. Bernard dog. Our train was late; at last it came in. LUckily we got an empty carriage to our- selves. When we got to Carstairs the line was flooded, and the water was up to the windows of the houses. Also the crops were spoilt by the rain, and I felt rather sorry for the unlucky , farmers. When we arrived at our destination it was pouring, and when we got home it was half-past one.—Your affectionate niece, DOROTMY BENNETT." Well, Dorothy, I daresay you are wondering why I have not given the name of the regiment of soldiers that you mention are stationed at the Bridge of Erne? The reason is that if I did a Big Bogey named The Censor (have you ever heard of him?) would come for poor old Uncle Ned, and perhaps take him off to prison. And what would you think if next Saturday, when you looked at the Corner, you just saw: "Notice. Uncle Ned has gone to prison for telling thino.s he oughtn't to !" Wouldn't you have a shock? And no Corner to read either!

REV. S. F. COLLIER'S SONS.

The Rev. Donald B. Collier, the.third son of the Rev. S. F. Collier, superintendent of the Manchester and Salford Wesleyan. Mission, was ordained at the Albert Hall on Monday night. The ordination was an emergency one, follow- ing on Mr. Collier's appointment to a chap- laincy to the ltorces in Gallipoli, an appoint- ment which terminates' his theological course at Didsbury College at the end of his first year. The Rev. S. F. Collier has three sons holding commissions in the Army. Two of them are serving in the Dardanelles. One of these was wounded in one of the earlier attacks, but has since rejoined his regiment, and the other is still training in England. The Rev. Dr. Waddy Moss, president of the Wesleyan Conference, gave the ordination charge.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

Rev. John Yonge Sends Thanks For Sympathy.

The usual meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A., held on Sunday afternoon, was presided over by the Secretary, Mr. B. Farrar, who read the follow- ing letter from the Rev. John Yonge, who was the first president of the Society, and who recently had the misfortune to break his wrist: "26, Ellesmere-road, August 9, 1915. Dear Mr. Farrar,—Thank you very truly for procur- ing and sending the welcome message from my old friends of the P.S".A. Please assure them that I value very ranch their kindly sympathy. It is a great encouragement to me to know that my unfortunate accident has awakened so many kindly feelings in so many friendly and loving hcarts. It is a precious reward ,for ,so many years of life and service 'at Wycliffe to receive evidences of continued interest and concern in my experiences, how that I am "out of harness" and living apart from active work. Will my friends of the P.S.A. be always confident that my interest in their welfare as a Brotherhood is still deep and constant. 1 regret to hear of diminished attendances, partly to be explained by the absence of so many "at the Front." But I cling to the hope that when this terrible season of conflict is over, and our men come home, we may see a great and wide religious revival, which may have a good effect upon our people generally, and may re-inspire our churches with more vigorous life! Let us all Limy that this may come to pass in God's good time !—Ever heartily yours, Jonx YONGE." BUSY AFTER BIG BATTLE.

Mr. Farrar also read the following letter from Sergeant Aaron Landor, a member, in barracks at Boulogne : " Dear friond,—l was very pleased to receive your letter, and also to hear that I am not for- gotten by my P.S.A. friends, for I have spent many happy hours as a member, and hope to spend a few more, as soon as this little job out here is finished. I don't think that is a long way off now either; but I can assure you that whenever the time comes it won't be too soon. I am in charge of a party of stretcher-bearers here, and our duty is to take the wounded on to the hospital ships; so we are kept rather busy, especially after a big battle. The people of Eng- land should be thankful that this war is not being fought over there, and that we stepped in when we did, for if we hadn't, it would very soon have been fought there." Still another message was read from Private G. T. Yeates, of New. Cottages, Orford. who is now wounded and in Worn Hospital, Bed. The brethren sent thei sincere sympathy to Mr. and Mrs. Hodson (parents of Brother Hod- son, now at the Front) in the death of their daughter.

SEEKING GOOD.

Mr, Jos. Hamblett, who gave the address upon "Seeking good and not evil," said that they should ever keep their eyes upon the good, in people, striving to bring it forth, ft,nd endur- ing; for it; and laying out their own lives for doing good, and being watchful against evil. At the present time they were called upon to save all they could, wasting nothing and not being extravagant. The rich, as well as the poor, would seek not luxury and extra enjoyment, but live plainly--eating to live and not living to eat. Messrs. A. Healey and Yearsley also iiirtici- pated in the meeting; whilst Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ.

NEW DRINKING ORDER FOR MERSEY DISTRICT.

First Day's Results.

A new Order curtailing drinking hours in the Merseyside district to five and a half daily came into fprce on Monday, when 12 noon was the first hour at which licknsed premises were open for the sale of intoxicating liquor. Inquiries late in the evening elicited the in- formation that if there had been any objection by the working :classes to -Shortened hours it had been of a passive character. Work at the docks and other places of activity proceeded as usual. During the hours when alcoholic bever- ages were saleable there was no undue rush. Several houses remained open during non- drinking hours for the supply of food and non- intoxicating refreshments.

LEATHER TRADE.

Busy Again After A Brief Holiday.

The trade last week made speedy recovery from the temporary holiday quiet-tide, and basi- ness was brisk wherever any of the usable lines were offering, writes the Warrington eorre- spondent of the "-Leather Trades Review." Although it is said there is not much in the way of lerray ordeDs being placed just now, there is evidently a desire on the part of eon- sumers to anticipate, tkeir needs to the end of the year, and contracts just expiring have been fairly freely reneircdt. It would seem as though recent arrangements regarding fixity of values for hides and leather have at least imparted a feeling of ecurity in. the permanence of pre- sent conditions, hence there appears a greater degree of freedom in buoring. Sole leather has been active all round_ It still remains difficult to supply Army leather as quickly as wanted, and users are regularly pressing for delivery as well as seeking for additional supplies to, make up for shortage in deliveries elsewhere. All the other weights frbm 121 b. upwards were in full demand, so much so that stocks are greatly depleted, and as a rule, Orders have now to await execution until fresh goods come along. Repeated in- quiry is made for light bends, to which there appears little response; the few there are to come have been sold long ago, and, from the class of pelt which has been going into the fan. yards during the past 12 months, I imagine the prospect of any considerable quantity coming along in the near future is very remote. . A strong demand also exists for a low-priced bend about 23d.-24d., but this, too, meets with little encouragement, although it might be possible to produce such good S from some of the foreign pelts offering. The difficulty is that tanners are already so full up with urgent work that very few could find either room or opportunity to cater for this lower traille. Shoulders of all classes are in strong demand; full prices are easily made, and the great difficulty is to avoid overselling.

Price Of Army Leather To Be Controlled. The Director of Army contracts has notified , the makers of harness, saddlery, and accoutre- ments that the price of leather is in future to be "controlled." The circular states, "It has been agreed by the members of the United Tanners' Federation of Great Britain and Ire- land, after a conference between representa- tives of the federation and this Department that the maximum prices they will charge for future sales of leather suitable for Army accoutrements, saddlery, harness, &c., shall not exceed an advance of 10 per cent on the whole hide on prices current during the first 14 days of May last. This agreement will hold good until the end of November, 1915. Prices at which leather will be sold after November will form the subject of a further agreement with the Tanners' Federation. Birmingham and Walsall makers of harness and saddlery, &c.,. have been exceedingly busy on Government contracts and the price of . leather has steadily advanced until exorbitant rates have had to be paid.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, ATJGUSe 21, 1915.

THE UNYIELDING HEALTH COMIVIITTEE.

What Mr. JOHIN Ursox calls the "unyield- ing attitude " of the Health Committee—. supported as they are by the Town Council— has caused a wholesale resignation of members from theXedical Inspection Committee of the Education Committee. The Education Com- mittee have to administer the Acts specially dealing with the health of school children. They pay the whole of the salary of the School Dental Officer, and five-sixths of the salary of the Assistant Medical Officer of Health, who is tho school doctor. They supervise and are responsible for the work of these gentlemen. say the Health Committee, "imitheug,lt the Education Authority do the work, we must keep all health officer appoint- ments in our own hands." The Medical In- spection Sub-committee do not see the force of this argument. To ask a committee to aceept responsibility for work without having authority Over the officers who do the work, is to—ask *lee much nowadays, and, as will be seen tram letters we publish te=day, Messrs. PAnztan, IrsoN and JARMAN, and Mrs. JACK- aOtf HOSABie have followed their Chairman's lead ,and resigned. How many others will fellow the same line of action we do not know, , but it is certain that the whole of the com- mittee, with perhaps one or two exceptions, claim th• right to make these appointments at least jointly with the Health Committee. They some time ago resolved, unanimously, wu believe, on the proposition of Mr. trrsox, , that %ha School Dental Officer should be appointed by the Inspection Committee, and the Assistant Medical Officer by a joint com- mittee' of the Health Committee and three members of. the Inspection Committee. This mild compromise is what the Health Com- mittee have rejected for some reason that is not clear. What is clear is that their stub- bornness is threatening to deprive the town of the services of men who are not only able but willing to do practical public health work. They have already achieved many valuable results. Why should this be checked? Councillor ROBERTS hopes that some means may be found whereby the valu- able serYices of Mr. KINGDON and his col-_

leagues may be continued for the benefit of

the health of our school children. We hope so, too. Perhaps now a crisis has been forced something reasonable will be done hir the Council

THE FOOT3ALL SEASON.

The Warrington Football Club has been sub- jeoted to some hostile criticism for not having fallen in with the suggestion that the North- ern Union League clubs should engage in friendly matches during the canting season. This criticism comes only from outside the town, and may be disregarded. So far as we can see there would he no difference between playing Leettue ntatehefs and friendly matches. The men and, the; " gates " would be as neces- sary for the one as for the other, and .the committee have done the right thing in not resorting to hypocrisy. It i finally and un- alterably decided that, till the end of the war, Wilderspool will not witness' football, except that played by youngsters. The Inter,. mediate League have resolved that players must be under la years of age next April, so that men of military age will have no chance of playing .11Rder. the League. 'kr. MEE: RS stated in our report of- the meeting of the Intermediate League, will bo glad to hear from clubs interested in the prog,rarame for the coming season.
