TO-MORROW IN THE PARK

To-morrow the great annual effort that is made by the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee to aid the Warrington Infirmary by means of 'lan Outdoor entertainment will take place for the second time under war condi- tions, and, for a second time, the main con- cern of the promoters will be the alleviation of pain and suffering caused by the war. Last year there were doubts as to the propriety of holding a "festival," but twelve months of serious contact with losses 'and hardships has tempered us against a desire to give ourselves over unduly to sadness, and to regard only the mournful side of the picture. As a matter of fact, the exertions that have been made en all hands to lighten the burdens of those prim- arily affected &y the conflict, by providing them with comforts of various kinds, will rank high among the many bright deeds that have been done to win the war. The pleasanter these efforts are the more good they are calcu- lated to achieve, for they give greater promise of diverting our minds from one depressing thought, of attracting a large volume of sup- port, and of bringing the largest possible amount of support for the funds that are so badly needing it. The open-air festival in the Park to-morrow will have all the elements of success. In the first place the choir, which will number 250 voices, has been drawn from the choirs of churches of all denominations--Anglican, Roman, and Free. Hitherto, only Free Church choirs have sustained the programme, and the innovation is especially appropriate slays when all classes—with the exception of a few miserable people who are making out- casts of themselves—are pulling together as one united body. In the second place we are to witness for the first time in Warrington the experiment of performing oratorio in the open- air. The combined choirs will render "The Messiah," a strong force of soloists having given their services. They will be supported by an efficient orchestra drawn from the best amateurs in town. The rehearsals have been well attended, and the results augur well for a successful performance. We hope the public wilt assure the GUCCeSS of the festival from a financial standpoint. If the weather is wet the festival will take place in Parr Hall.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
-AND IN THE HIPPODROME.

As last year, the open-air festival will be supplemented by an evening concert in the Hippodrome, where, at rwarter-past eight, the Band ,of the 2nd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment will begin to discourse sweet music. The festival and the concert last year got together a substantial sum, and, as special stress is beinglaid this year on the needs of our local men in the Dardanelles, both on land and sea, the proceeds should be larger

In view of the many engagements that the splendid band of the 2nd Battalion have ful- filled in this district for patriotic and charit- able objects since the war began, they ought to receive a hearty welcome to-morrow night. Two of its members have won the D.C.M. in this campaign, as was reported in "The Examiner" a few weeks since. The pro- gramthe they will submit will be an attractive one. Fletcher's "Spirit of Pageantry" is a prize production; Le Thiere, the composer of "Gipsy Life," was for many years a bands- Man in the Coldstream Guards, and has pro- duced many tuneful pieces; the "Gems of Verdi" will embrace Verdi's best known works.

THE EXAMINER, S ATURDAY, AUGUST 21, 1915.

SCHOOL MEDICAL WORK. 11YESLEYAN MINISTER'S WEDDING.

RESPONSIBILITY WITHOUT AUTHORITY.

Mr. Charles E. Parker Resigns From

Medical Inspection Committee.

Last week we recorded the fact that Mr. Holman Kingdon had resigned the chairman- ship of the Medical Inspection Committee of the Education Committee because the Town Council had supported the claim of the Health Committee to the right of making all appoint- ments of officials, irrespective of the opinion of the members of the Inspection Committee, who have to administer the Acts. Mr. Chas. E. Parker, another member of the aggrieved com- mittee, has reSigned, the following being copy of a letter he has this week sent, to the (hair- man. of the Health Committee:—

Penketh. near Warrington, August 16th. 1915. Henry Roberts. Esq., Kingsley, Hill Cliff e.

Dear Mr. Roberts,—l sec by the local Press that the Town Council hatfe decided to support the Health 'Committee in their determination to reserve to themselves the appointment of all the officers who conduct the duties for which the Medical Inspection Committee is responsible. Tinder these cireumstances I feel that I 'have no option, and would ask you kindly to place my resignation as a member Of the Medical In- potion Committee before the Education Com- mittee at their next meeting. It is unnecessary to enter into the reasons which compel me to take this action. These have been fully giyen in the varioni discussions on the question,. and I see are clearly oidgiM 4 Mr. Kingdon in his letter to yourself. The position taken by the Health Committee, and endorsed by ,the Council, gives me no choice in this matter..

I quite recognise the obligation which rests on every citizen to take his part in public service, and I have no wish to shirk this duty. Vrappily there are ample opportunities for pub- lic service in directions, and under conditions, in which an individual can maintain his in- terest and self-respect, and in which he may hope to make his service effective. In this case where responsibility is imposed and authority denied this seems to me impossible. Yours very truly,

CHARLES E. PARKER.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. UPSON RESIGNS.

The Vain Hope Of A Reasonable Compromise.

Mr. J. 'Upson, the teachers' representative on the Education Committee, has written to the Chairman as follows :—,

8, Lodge-lano, Warrington, August 18th, 1915. Dear Mr. Roberts,—l beg to place in your hands my resignation as a member of the Medical Inspection Committee. As you know I have from the first taken a very decided POsition against the action of the Health Committee in arrogating to itself the solo right of making the appointments of the officers for school medical work, tne whole or nearly tho whole of whose salaries are charged against the Education Committee. I have only remained a member of the com- mittee so long in the hope—the vain hope it now proves—that some reasonable compromise might be arranged between the two committees, but the unyielding attitude of the, Health Com- mittee and the Council makes it impossible for self-respecting men to serve upon a committee the members of which are held directly respon- sible by the Government for the work of medical inspection and, all that that ifoplies, while at the same time they are denied the 'right of having any voice in the appointment of the officers who kave to carry out that work, and for whose work they have to take responsibility. Yours faithfully, JOHN PSON.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. JARMAN VACATES EATEN-

ABLE POSITION.

Mr. Jai:hes Jarmano who repreents the War- rington Friendly Societies, has also resigned. Writing to-Couneillor Roberts he says:- 78, Diancheeter-road, Warrington, August 18, 1915.

Dear Sir,—ln view of the decision of the Coun- cil respecting medical inspection appointments and the action of the Chairman, Mr. Kingdon, in resigning, I cannot see that any other coarse is open to those of us who agree with his action than to also resign our positions on that com- mittee.

I regret therefore to ask yon kindly to re- move my name from the Medical Inspection Sub-committee.

Yours respectfully. J. JARMAN.

MRS. HOLMES SUPPORTS HER CHAIRMAN.

.. Mrs. Jackson Holmes has written to the Chairman of the Education Committee :

Barnacre, Latchford Without, Warrington. August 20, 1915.

Dear Mr. Roberts,—As our worthy Chairman of the Medical Inspection Committee cannot retain his position on account of the decision of the Town Council to uphold the action of the Health Committee, I feel obliged to support him and hereby tender my i'esignation as a member of that sub-committee. Yours sincerely, MARY E. HOLMES.

DEATH OF COLONEL WOOD,

OF NEWTON.

On Monday Colonel Joseph L. Wood, of Newton-le-Willows, who was born August 3rd, 1847, and has thus just completed his 88th year, died suddenly of heart disease. Colonel Wood worked himself up from the bottom of the ladder to the position of manager at the print- ing works of Messrs. McCorquodale. He was made a justice of the peace for Lancashire in 1893; and, taking a great interest in the Volunteer movement, was the holder of the Volunteer Decoration.

The funeral took place on Thursday, inter- ment in Newton-le-Willows Cemetery following a service at St. Peter's Church. The body was carried to its last resting place by Col.-Sergt. T. Appleton, .Sergt. J, Runnis and Sergt. F. Appleton, of the 6th Volunteer King's Liver- pool Regiment, who served with the Colonel, Q.M.S. M. Reigate (Lanes. Hussars), Mr. Robert Moon (gardener), Mr. Chas. Meakin (-coachman). There was a large number of wreaths. A detachment of the Lancashire Police from Warrington and Wigan Divisions, in charge of Inspectors Clarke and Smith, followed. The mourners included Mr. Leslie Hargreave Wood, (son), Mr. Jas. Simpson, of " Parkstone," Dorset (brother-in-law), Lieut. Ardern R. Wood (nephew), Mr. Geoffrey Simp- son (nephew), Mr. William Clarke, of Lowton (cousin), Mr. J. A. Clarke, of Eccles (cousin), Dr. Harold Watkins. Mr. Aubrey White (solicitor), Mr. Alfred Nickels, of Birkenhead; Mr. Wm. Tinsley, Miss Clark, Mrs. Alfred Nickels, Mr. Langshaw Williams, .a West Kirby; Mr. Robt. Stone, of Newton Park; Mr. Bradshaw (representing Mr. Henry Greenall), and maids from the deceased's residence.

Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, Warrington and Liverpool, carried out the funeral arrange- ments.

Captain Halsall Owen's Will .- Captain Halsall Owen, of Buifield, Apple- ton, Cheshire, who died on June 28th, left net personalty £5,576 7s. 6d., and gross £5,815 as. 7d. He was the second son of the late Mr. William Owen, J.P., architect. Probate has been granted to dec,eased's brother, Mr. Segar Owen, of Kelniscott, Appleton. architect and surveyor, who is one of the executors.

THE REV. HUGH WILLIAMSON MARRIES MISS MARION WEAVER.

In the presence of a large congregation, Miss Marion Weaver, daughter of Mr: and Mrs. George Weaver, of Springfield-street, Warring- ton, and the Rev. Hugh Williamson, son of Mr. John Williamson, a Laurel Bank White- haven, were married at Bald-street Bank, Church on Tuesday....morn,ing. From a few minutes before the time 'fixed for the com- mencement of the service till the confetti.. flinging climax of the ohurch eereniony, there was a considerable crowd outside the building. The bridegroom was until *Sunday evening, when he preached his farewell sermon, pastor of the Bold-street Church.

The bride was attired in ivory satin ,and ninon. She had a square train, with bride's initial embroidered in pearls. Orange blossom, white heather, .and a shower bouquet of pink roses and lilies Of the valley completed the toilet. She was attended by two bridesmaid—the

Sunday evening, to mark his departure for an- other sphere, of duty, after completing, four years in. the Warrington circuit. Prior to de- livering his- sermon, the rev. gentlemaut ex- pressed his regret at leaving his friends of the Bold-street Church. Ills chief relationship to the majority of them had been that of a friend; before anything else. And the part of a friend was always a pleasant part to play. He could not leave that circuit without expressing his thanks to the splendid band of helpers who had assisted him in his activities. There was no church in Methodism with a more loyal lot of helpers; and there was no minister in Metho- dism who had been more heartily supported. wished there had been more of them, but those who had helped him had done so splen- didly.. MUSICAL FESTIVAL AND CONCERT.

To-Morrow In Park And Hippodrome. ,

The combined choirs of the:, dhurches in Warrington and an orchestra of local 'instru- mentalists will perform Handel's oratorio, "The Messiah," in Bank Park to-morrow after- noon at three o'clock. Among the soloists are Mesdames Sara Silcock; Jennie Armstrong, E. A. Sanders; 'Misses El sio Hewitt, May Hocken- hall; .Messrs. W. Booth, P. Goostrey, 114 Pearce and W..ll.,Rigby. Mr. T. Bradford Barton is leader of. the orchestra, Mr. H. L. Read organist and .accompanist, and Mr. W. H. Payton is conductor. There will be no charge for ad- mission, but there, will be a collection at the gates and a .small .payment will be expected. for the privilege of 'using a limited number" Of' seats. In case of wet weather the festival will be transferred to Parr Hall.

BRIDEGROOM.

we are more than conquerors through Him that loved us. For I am persuaded that neither death nor life, nor angels, nor principalities, nor powers, nor things present, nor things to come, nor height, nor depth, nor any other creature, shall be able to separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord." Space, Temperament and Selfishness.

How good it would be, he said, if we could say "nothing can separate us,". and leave it at that! How good it would be if we could look out upon the world of men, now unfortunately divided in a lerrible war, and say "nothing can separate us!" How good it would be if the spirit of Brotherhood had fascinated the world, instead of. the spirit that now ruled! But, alas! The day far those things had not yet arrived. There were -many things that worked for separa- tion. Distance separated. If it did not, how many of theni 'would be that -ziening in, France or' Flanders with loved ones. Then there was temperament that separated. There were some people with whom one could not get on. They lived in their world and the others lived in an- other world. One of the best illustrations of the point was in nationality. How far the different nationalities were apart! Selfishness was a third thing that could and did separate.' It was the future to which we must look forward, when, with the advance of science; space might be annihilated, leading to the universal brother- hood of man.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
"More Than A Conqueror."

The Rev. H. Williamson went on to apply his remarks to the. question.of separation fron:i God. 'Nothing. he said, (timid separate us from the love of. God, but _selfishness, could stand in the way for a long time. God wanted everyone to trituntih right through life.. He wanted them all to be glorious successes. He wanted them all to say, "I am more than a conqueror." It was his prayer that every one of them would be more than a conqueror for their own sake and for the sake of the God who meant them to triumph.

The grand and well-known circus of Sir Robert Fossett and Sons'—hippodrome and animal combination—is touring this district with much success. REGISTRATION.

To-day The Last Day For Completion Of Initial Work.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
TEN THOUSAND PINK

FORMS.

AR "Examiner" man yesterday morning interviewed Mr. W. H. Woodcock, assistant borough collector, whom ho sought out in order to obtain information on the subject of the taking of the National Register locally.

SIXTEEN THOUSAND HOUSES VISITED. He was told that altogether a hundred and fifty enumerators were employed on the work, half of thAnn,women and half men. The work commenced on Monday morning and the boxes began to come v in on Tuesday. Yesterday morning the whole of the boxes with the ex- ception of fourtoen—representing about a tenth of the local registration—had been received. To-day is the last day for the boxes to be returned from the enumerators. Approximately 16,000 houses were visited.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
LADIES KINDLY RECEIVED.

The lady enumerators have remarked as to how kindly they have been received. They state that they have invariably met with great courtesy and great willingness by the public to fill up the forms. There were two cases of people refusing to fill up the forms, but they were referred to the Town Clerk and amicably settled, the forms being subsequently filled in. Each of the nine wards was -oovered by a number of enumerators varying according to its size. For instance, enumerators, Ward had nine enumerators and Latchford twentyLsix.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFICIALS.

All' the collectors were volunteers. The Town Clerk is answerable for the work, but a lot of the responsibility has devolved upon Mr. Woodcock, assisted by Mr. Elliott (deputy director of education), Mr. J. McAdam (assistant librarian), Mr. 11. Lawrence and Mr. G. Rice (assistants in the Borough Treasurer's office). The ward supervisors_ were: Coun- cillor Henry Roberts, and Messrs. Herbert Gibson, F. S. Rossiter„ S. P. Sikook, F. G. Ruddock, A. S. Elliott, J. McAdam, G. W. A. Brown, and Dr. G. W. N. Joseph (medical officer of health). ' •

===== 0002601_19150821_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PINK FORMS.

The pink forms--those which will be sent to the Military Authorities, bearing the names of men eligible for service with the4orces—will number about ten or twelve thousand. There will be a sufficient snpply to keep the recruit- ing ataff busy visiting for about a. week. The work qf classification will be com- meneed on Monday, and it is anticipated that the male classifications will be completed dur- ing the week, and the females during the ensuing week.' The men will be classified into forty-six divisions, according to the nature of their employment, and the females into thirty divisions. It is expected that by the 11th of September the whole of the work will have been completed. Certificates are being prepared, and each per- son registered will receive one within the course of a few days. About fifty thousand certificates will be issued. The enumerators will deliver the certificates.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
REGISTRATION HUMOUR.

Some Notes By A Local Enumerator.

The varying receptions accorded to the enumera- tor tax his versatility and diplomacy to the limit. Mostly he is viewed- with suspicion, again he will be enthusiastically greeted by a well- meaning householder who apparently imagines that effusiveness and embarrassing anxiety to "do what's right" prove his patriotism. Sometimes ho is merely tolerated, and, worst of all, occasionally contemptuously asked "how is it that you toffs get all the jobs when there's ' quid' or two at back on it," or "no wonder at rates going up wasting brass keeping you chaps walking about." On the whole, however, the householders have accepted the inevitable with a very good grace.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
* * * * * *

Should these lines meet the eye of any of the parties who unconsciously played a part in the following scenes I assure thei:n that wild horses could not drag the names from me; also that they are one and all forgiven even if ray dignity did get many nasty jars such as for instance when a parcel was dropped at my feet with just the remark "Laundry," and the door slammed before I could make reply.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
*** * * *

Ono lady informed me that if I was calling next door I should have to knock until the dog barked, as "the old woman's deaf." The door opened after the dog had done his duty and "the old woman" regarded me with a frankly hostile stare. She was deaf. Trouble now commenced. I had literally to howl in,her ear; every time I yelled the dog went for me, greatly to the delight of the juveniles who had been attracted by the row. The enumerator who successfully fulfills his mission there will require to be a human centipede. It takes some .doing to hold a box, book, pencil, fill up a form, shout in "the old woman's" ear and kick a dog at one and the same time.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
* . * * * 0 *

A gentleman at No -, had duly filled up his form, and in Column 6 was entered "Timber Merchant." After many inquiries as to whether he imported timber. or if used for building purposes, &c., &c., I coaxed the un- willing information that he had a handcart and sold firewood. I don't think he will ever forgive me. He looked upon my action as per- sonal spite. ,

===== 0002601_19150821_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
*** * * *

A little farther up the street a maiden of same twelve summers answered the door. Indigna- tion showed all over her face and, before I could speak she turned her back to me, and in shrill tones shouted, " Mother! there's a hawker stood on't step wet you've just mopped." A few words from " mother " considerably altered my ideas as to the uses of doorsteps, from now henceforth they are "taboo."

*** * * *

Four papers had been left Wine at No. —, and only one was handed back. The reply to my request for an explanation was that "Our 'a companion has taken the other three." I pointed out that only duly author- ised persons were entitled to deal with the papers, but the old lady left me limp when she smilingly assured me that "It's all right; friend is working on the 'Ree-gester' —she's an .` illuminator.' Doubtless the friend will be able to throw light on problems that would baffle the mere male.

One youth was in 'trouble regarding "If born abroad, &c."; he didn't know what to a.nawer. I asked : "Are you a British subject?" and he thought he was., "Were you born in this country?" was ,my next query. "N 0. he replied, "I was born in Hanley!" I * .* * * * *

"How many persons have you living here between the ages of 15 and CZ?" was my usual method of opening the attack. A burly forge- man met the onslaught and wiped ma out when he said:• "Somewheer abart eight— there's me an' th' owd woman, an' one lad at Crosfield's, two we buried at Rochdale, an' a dowter' at's wed to a 'sowjer,' but hoe lives at Sankey wi' his mother."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
*** * * *

Much more could be told. Now I wish to hand over a word of sympathy to the classi- fiers who have to deal with the multitude of the forms. Forms there are of all kinds— some of which have been filled up with scrupulous care, others where every effort seems to have been used to make them unde- cipherable; a few have apparently been used either as a plate or a cover for the popular "chips and fish," 'whilst a glorious disregard to a direct answer of the questions is a pro- minent feature. The ladies particularly are the worst sinners in this respect, and I fear that the classifiers will find the female "form" very much removed from "divine."

After a man finally finds an ideal woman she passes him up because she le looking for an ideal man.

Births and Deaths.

In the week ending August 14th there were 40 births in Warrington against , 51 in the corrb- sponding week last year. The deaths numbOred 20 against 19. Cancer and pneumonia accounted for three each of the deaths. Scarlet fever provided 23 hoesh cases.

Mr. A. J. Thorp, 160, Manchester-road, has writtea a tribute to General Botha, and, hav- ing sent a sow to the King and to Queen Alexandra, has received acknowledgments from both Buckingham Palace and Marlborough House. It would well befit your station And your master soldier-hand, If we Galled the conquered nation Like Rhodesia, "Botha's Land."

GOll2lanf or _Drink

Last night was S-ons of Temperance night with the' Warrington Citizen' Temperance League, and a successful meeting was held at the foot of Bowsoy-street. Bro. W. Burrows, of St. Helens, presided, and the speaker was Bro. R. Mclntyre (N.D. ~organiser), who asked: Is drink a friend or an enemy? He pointed out, that nearly all the belligerent countries in this war had taken stops either to abolish or curtail the drink traffic. Russia had stopped the manu- facture of Yedke; France had abolished the production of absinthe; and in England we had curtailed the hours of trade. Apparently, there was a unanimity of opinion as be whether drink was a friend or an enemy. Th• speaker said be did not know whether Germany or drink was doing us a greater amount of harm to-day. He thought if the matter were carefully probed, Germany would come out a bad second.

Child Drowned in the Canal. Coroner Brighouse conducted an inquest in the Warrington Coroner's Court on Monday, an the body of Edith Ellen Hodson, six years of. age, daughter of Mr. Fred Hodson, local secre- tary of the National Inion of Dock Labourers, who resides at 239, Liverpool-road. The father said that on returning from busi- ness on Thursday evening of last week he was told that his daughter had gone out with a girl named Lily Allen, and had not returned. He ' searched for her in vain, whereupon he went to Alien's home, but the girl was in bed, and was too drowsy for any information to be got from her. The pollee were notified, and, the next morning lie interviewed Lily, who said that while playing her friend had fallen into the canal. Dragging operations were commenced. with the result that the girl's body was dis- covered on Friday evening. The jury returned a verdiot of "Accidentally drowned."

A Good Ambulance Class. Candidates to qualify for ambulance work continue to Come forward in gratifying num- bers in Warrington, and judging from tho result of the examinations they appear to applY themselves to their work with commendable zeal. The latest formed clasd, held under the auspices of the Warrington Headquarters Division of the Brigade was instructed by Dr. J. S. Manson, the divisional surgeon. Of 2S, who presented themselves for examination a few evenings ago at the Police Station, 27 passed; Dr. L. M. Prank Christian, of Stock- port, one of the best known ambulance men is the country being the examiner. The candidates who passed are eligible for service either naval or military, at home or abroad. So far this month Private J. GregerY• from Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, has joined the Royal Naval Sick Berth Reserve at Chat- ham, and Private S. Quinn. from Johnson.'" Basket Works, Black Bear Bridge, has bees sent to the Military Hospital at Tidworth.

Bewsey Adult School Horticulturists. Saturday last was a big day for the Beirsel Adult School, by reason of the fact that school's first flower show was held. Some little while back the school leased a fairly larg• piece of waste land from the Cheshire Line° Railway Committee, and they have since con- verted the land to good purpose, the memberg having taken up allotments for the cultivation of flowers and vegetables, and, in addition, a bowling green having been laid out. Mr. Upton, the florist, kindly consented tg judge the exhibits, and he was quite Pleasea with the results of the -work of the member!), both men and women, and was loud in hie praises.

Mrs. T. Pemberton*, of Bewsey, presented the prizes, and expressed the opinion that th• hobby of which that afternoon's show was , reflection was an admirable one, affording. a 9 it did, considerable pleasure and edification. The prize-winners and those who were highlY recommended for their exhibits were :--3efils' Boyle and Mrs. Whalley, Messrs. Sam ton, Charles Charles Starkey, Ben Sproston,' Bouchier and John Elwell. Tea was served in the Pavilion, a building , constructed by certain of the members. The/ have also made a greenhouse and a summer; house, and next year it is hoped the g 1151141 will afford much pleasnrable social intercouraa and recreation.

WOUNDED.

PRIVATE H.

lIIPKISS.

Merniwr Of Orford Tannery Football Club.

Private Herbert Hipkiss, who lives With his wife at. 47, Scott-street, was wounded in the head with shrapnel .on the 9th July. On Sun- day morning Mrs. Hipkiss received the follow- ing post-card from her husband :—" I am going

in hospital on Friday to undergo an operation. It is not serious, so don't upset yourself." Private Hipkiss, who is at prefent a member of "D" Company, rejoined the Territorials on the outbreak of war, having .previously served for eight years in the regiment. He is em- ploygd in peace times' by, Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Bank Quay. He has played football, several times for the Orford Tannery team.

Private Taylor was quite safe and well ou June 16th.

Sacred Heart Scholar.

Aged 23, Private R. Taylor was born in War- rington, and as a boy attended Sacred Heart School, afterwards going to work as a butcher with his uncle, Mr. Tom Higham, Liverpool- road. Later he took up farming, and after some months entered as a wiredrawer the service of the Whitecross Company. On the outbreak of war, however, he joined the Territorials, going out to the Front early in February. He wrote home regularly. . in times of peace he attended the Friars' Green Bible Class.

The Private has three brothers engaged on Government work.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 21, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE W.

GIBSON.

Iteported By Comrades To Have Been

Buried.

There is still no definite news concerning Private William Gibson, son of Mr. James Gibson, plumber ,and painter, Mill-street. It will be remembered that shortly after the great charge on the 16th June, a private in the Middlesex Regiment; wrote to Mr. Gibson, telling him that his son had been killed, and that he had helped to bury him. Letters to this effect have also been received from other soldiers at the Front. He is reported from the War Office as wounded and missing, having previously been reported as wounded.

- Private Gibson joined the Territorials in Sep tember, and , was allocated to R Company. Before offering his services he worked with his

===== 0002601_19150821_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
father as a plumber and painter. Ile received his education at the People's College, Arpley- street, and he formerly belonged to the choir of St. Paul's Church, being also greatly inter- ested in the company of the Church Lads' Bri- gade associated with that parish. He was a member of ,the Borough Band, and also of the Cheshire Lines Band, and attended -the Young Men's Bible Class at St. Pail'.

WILL YOU HELP

Our "Terriers" In Training To Be Healthy And Happy?

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—May I, through your columns, appeal to Warrington to help us in liroviding amuse- ments for inen of this battalion stationed at Weeton Camp? _ -

Weeton is 4. miles away from anywhere," and except for a small picture palace in the village and the Y.M.C.A. tent in camp, there is little recreation for the men after work. A committee has therefore been formed to encourage football, cricket, boxing, Le., and if we can once get these sports started the men will enjoy many a pleasant evening which they would otherwise employ in wandering about the roads.

May I, as President of the Sports Committee, appeal to our friendsrin Warrington and New- ton-le-Willows for donations either in money or kind?

Footballs, cricket bats, cricket balls and boxing gloves, either new or second-hand, will be just as acceptable as stamps, postal orders, bank notes and cheques, and I feel confideAt that this appeal to the ever-generous public in Warrington and district will not be in vain.— Yours, &c.,

BRIAN T. DICKSON, LIEUT. 9/4th Battalion South Lancs. Regt, Weeton Camp, near Preston.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
HEROISM!

Warrington Territorial's Tribute

To His Comrades.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FAIREST SPOT ON

EARTH.

Councillor llenry Roberts has received from a personal friend of his, who is a non-com- missioned officer in the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, a letter from which he has made the following extracts for us:— " You speak in your letter of the heroism of our soldiers. I speak from what I have seen and know, and it is really wonderful. I have seen men mortally wounded; men with great gaping wounds, shockingly mutilated, stagger like drunken men, struggling magnificently and refusing help to the. nearest dressing station— always smiling; never a grumble—oh, it was a sight for the gods! . . " Everywhere "around (this refers to the famous, action on the 16th of June last) seemed like Hell let loose, and one wonders how any- thing alive came through it all. I have had many years' service in the Volunteers and Territorials, and many a time, after giving lip practically all my spare time have been subject to insult 'and abuse. I know I shall not appeal in vain when I ask you to defend our lads against any such abuse. Warrington has' no need to be ashamed of her, sons. They have played their part like reai Britishers, and you need have no. tesitation in saying they, are equal to the best out here. . .

===== 0002601_19150821_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
"This War Has Made Many A Man."

"This is a land of death and desolation Graves everywhere; thousands of them scat- tered about. Nothing but just a rough cross. probably made out of an old biscuit-box with a rough inscription in pencil. I should say it is an. impossibility to find an agnostic or atheist among the actual fighting troops. Some of them will swear like troopers, but in the hour of trial a silent prayer. goes up to the God of Battles. This war has made many,,,a man, and some of the things he counted great have become as dross. Some have given all, even life itself--but have kept their souls. . .

"As you say it is equality in munitions that we require, and I am happy to say we are fast approaching that. We can now wait patiently for the day when the tyrant with the mailed fist shall be brought down and riglit will conquer over might. . .

===== 0002601_19150821_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
Loveliest Of All.

"My heart goes out to our womenfolk at home. They are the real sufferers, and the more I travel the more I learn that the women of England are the purest and loveliest of all their sisters, and our dear old England (God bless and protect it) is the fairest and grandest spot on God's earth; a land worth living for and a land worth dying for."

ALUMINIUM WORKS' OVATION.

A most interesting event took place at the works of the British Aluminium Co., Ltd., at mid-day on Thursday, the occasion beinc, made the subject of a 'presentation to Corporal Tombs, V.C., of a gold watch and knife by his old colleagues at the works. The presentation was very ably made by Mr. Cash, the foreman of the mill, who, in some well chosen remarks, complimented the Corporal on his self-sacrifice and devotion to the service of his King and country.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Harry Picleersgill acted .as chairman

The Corporal, in his reply, 'warmly thanked his audience for the kind gifts ana the spirit which had actuated the givers. Ile assured his hearers that they would always bring back to him most pleasant memories whilst on service with the Forces, and also when he returned; if God ordained he should do so.

The Corporal was the recipient of a rousing ovation at the close.

THE CORPORAL AND TRADE ' UNION RULES.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
leVorkers Must Supply Soldiers.

A week's _recruiting campaign, organised on behalf of the West Lancashire Territorial Asso- ciation, was opened .at Bolton on Monday. Througheut the .da.y three bands, accompanied by detachments of troops, paraded the streets, which, despite the heavy showers bf rain, were lined with spectators, The feature of the day was the unexpected appearance of Corporal Tombs, Warrington, of the King's (Liverpool) Regiment. In asking the young men to become candi- dates for one of "these" .(the Cross, which hung suspended from his breast); he told the story of his visit to Btickingham Palace. After he received the decoration, he was stopped by an officer, who inquired, how he won the distinc- tion. "By doing my duty," Tombs replied, as he saluted.

Addressing a meeting of munition employes, he said workers must net let the soldiere stop for the want of material. Trade. unionism was all very well in its place, but inside the unions there were little circles who wouki meet and say they would turn out just so many shells per week and no more. That was a, very wrong thing to do. The men who agreed to do such a thing were a disgrace to the union they be- longed to. To stop, making shells when a certain amount had been turned out meant that they 'were stopping the soldiers from advancing.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
V.C. TOMBS.

Corporal Tombs heard his comrades cry There in the shrapnel thunder-storm, Stept from his trench and with courage high Brought back safely the bleeding form Of-a fellow-soldier and dragged him in Out of the hissing hail of death; Little he thought of the honours he'd win, But his comrades watched him and held their breath.

Came a cry on the wind once more; Corporal Tombs was quick to hear; Louder than all the battle's roar The voice of Duty rang in his ear. Bullets hissed, but he heeded not; Duty called and a friend lay prone; Through the shower of shell and the 'hail of shot, With heart undaunted, he -went alone;

And he seized his comrade and held him fast, And• dragged him out of the thunder's wrath: Surely the hero will pause at last; But he turned once more to the bloody path, And he went again, with his life in his hands, Through the flare and the fire, for another cries— The Corporal hears him and understands— And the leaden death once more defies.

God be praised! he has pulled him through Into the trench—'twas a gallant deed— And ever the deadly danger grew, But little the Corporal seemed to heed. The hottest fire of the foemen now Is aimed at the figure which does not quail; Death is a terror which cannot cow The man whose heart is a suit of mail.

All men wondered, for once again Straight into hell the Corporal leapt; Crawled through the blood-red rifle rain, Over the festering corpses crept, Seized a man who lay bleeding there— This was the fourth he had saved that day— Dragged him back tO the trenches bare And turned to his work in the usual way.

"Tombs," quoth his chief, "you're a gentle- man!" "It was but my duty," the Corporal said; But the fame of the deed through the regi- ment ran And on to the King of England sped; And a cross shall rest on the Corporal's breast,. And our hearts beat faster, and proud were we, As he came to the town we love the best, To welcome Corporal Tombs, V.C. ARTHUR BE_NNETT.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
PRISONERS OF WAR.

The work of dispatching comforts to the men of the South Lancashire Regiment who are in- terned as prisoners of war in Germany i pro- gressing steadily. Letters and postcards are being received daily from the men, acknowledging the parcels, and the tone of the communications shows how keenly the gifts are appreciated.' , It may interest some of our readers to know that nearly two ton weight of foodstuffs, over 20,000 cigarettes, and one cwt.. of tobacco- have already been dispatched from the Parr Hall. Every penny contributed is for the benefit of the prisoners. One example of this week's letters will, we think, suffice I received ,two parcels from you, and they were in (rood condition. The other men have received them quite safely. I am sure, Sir, that everybody of our regiment is grateful for your kindness in doing your best for us. It shall never be forgotten, especially by me, because I am an orphan, and it is the first I have had."

WAR ITEMS.

A SOLDIER'S FUNERAL.

The funeral of ths, late Bombardier Henry Hardman, of 82, Birchall-street, who met his death by falling over a cliff while in training with the, Royal Garrison Artillery on the East Coast, took place with full military honours last week. A cortege was formed by a• large detachment of his regiment, While the coffin, which was borne on a gun-carriage, was cov- ered with ths Union Jack, on which were placed the late Bombardier's cap, bandolier and spurs. Following came his horse with empty saddle, while in the stirrups were placed Bombardier Ha.rdman's riding boots. Included in a large number of beautiful floral tributes were wreaths from the Colonel and the late Bombardier's sergeant, and a cross from his comrades. The grave was adorned with lilies by the people of the neighbourhood. The relatives who attended the funeral in- cluded the mother, .widew, two children and three sisters.

ON WAR SERVICE.

in our last issue we reported that there had been sunk the Warrington steamship Summerfield—a steel screw three- masted steamer of 687 tons, built in 1913, and owned by the Zillah Shipping and Carrying Company, Limited, and registered at Liverpool. At the time of the sinking the chiefs of the crew were: Captain G. P. Hardley, Presten ; Thomas Summerfield, Garston, mate; and Owen Griffiths, single, chief engineer. Captain Hard- * was rescued and conveyed to hospital, in- jured, though not seriously. The mate, his wife, and the chief engineer were'drowned. Seven of the orew were landed, and two sailors, badly injured, were taken to hospital for treatment. The Zillah Company was originally a War-

After a short interval during which he served with his father, Mate Summerfield rejoined the company on the Wini- fred, which was sold nine months afterwards. Accordingly he transferred to the Zillah, a month later to the Aquilla, and so, on March 10, 1913, to the Sammerfield—on which he had since remained, with the exception only of three months, during which he studied for his certifi- cates.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
"BOTH LOST."

After the sinking a the vessel, the firm at once forwarded the following letter to the mate's father:—

Dear Captain Summerfield,—We are deeply distressed to have, to tell you that the s.s. Summerfield was sunk and your son Tom and his wife were both lost. It will be a great blow to you we know, but we hope you will find some consolation in the fact that your son died in a great cause, and as surely doing his duty as any soldier on the battlefield. We trust you will be given the necessary strength to sustain you in your bereavement of both son and daughter at one time. Any further news we may receive we will let you know at once. We are, yours faithfully, W. A. Savage, Ltd., H. Brough.

The mate's and his wife's last word home was contained in a postcard message whioh he posted only an hour or two before the vessel was sunk. The message ran as follows : My dear father and all at home,--Just a few lines hoping you are quite well, as it leaves us both. Love to all, from your loving son

and daughter. It is understood that at the time of sinking the mate was seen on the deck of the vessel, but was heard nothing more of—for he went to find his wife, and probably perished with her. The deceased mate—who was most highly respected by his employers and friends—leaves, besides his father, two 6iiters and a brother-in- law. to mown Wm.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
LINER SUNK.

Warrington Traveller's Escape.

A GERMAN'S WARNING.

The Arabic, a White Star liner, was tor- pedoed and sunk on Thursday. She sailed from Liverpool for New York, with a large number of passengers, on Wednesday. She was torpedoed without notice, and sank in eleven minutes. Of those on board 375 are known to have been saved, leaving 40 to be accounted for.

Mr. Edwin Knox, on of Mr. Thomas Knox, 12, Beresford-street, Warrington, just escaped being on board the Arabic. He is a commercial traveller for a Canadian firm with headquar- ters at Montreal, and came over to England seven weeks ago for a holiday. He same by the Arabic and was to have returned by the same boat this week, but was detained in London on the firm's business over that date. He began his return jeurney yesterday. _ _ _ _

Before he left Canada Mr. Knox was asked by a German the name of the boat by which he was travelling. On stating that he was booted by the Arabic, the German advised him not to go by that boat as the Germans had decided to sink her, along with another' vessel he, named.

All Mr. Knox's business papers and other belongings have gone down. with the Arabia..

HAS THE TIME COME FOR AMERICA "TO ACT?"

New York, Friday.—The morning papers here and throughout the country bitterly I:condemn

the sinking of the Arabic. - The "Tribune" writes: In every detail the 'attack fulfils President Wilson's definition of an act deliberately unfriendly. The time has come now to act. To talk further is to en- courage and not avoid murder. Washington, Friday.—Government secret agents suspect that large German purchases of war materials have been made and secretly stored for the equipment of Germans in the States in the event of hostilities with Germany.

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

WOUNDED.

RIFLEMAN W. H. HOWARN.

Motor-Builder And Scout Meets Good

Friend.

News has reached Mrs. Howard, 3g.., avenue, that that her son.—Rifleman William 11,orreby Howard, of the Rifle Brigade—has be rather severely wounded. It was first received from the headquarters of the Rifle Brigade at Wiz- ehester, while later the following let-ber was received from "Army Scripture Rosier C. Howard :

"At the request of your son, I am sending you a few lines. You will be grieved to know that he has been wounded rather severely. yet you will be reassured to know that he is making fair progress. In a day or two I hope So write to you again and let- ycra know how he is, and think the news will be encouraging. He rends his best love and wishes. May God genii-fart you in your anxiety and restore the dear lad to health again, is my prayer."

Hit in Head and Shoulder.

Later, Rieman Howard himself wret, the following letter from the 23rd General Hospital, France :

"I am getting on as well as earl be expected:

. . . I have been hit in the head and shoulder, and both are shrapnel wounds. I could not write any sooner. /suppose you got the letter from a good friend whom I met in hospital? I got wounded on Sunday morning, and we only went to the trenches on Saturday I wish I was in England." Rifleman Howard, who is seventeen years of age, enlisted shortly- after the outbreak of iy,ar. He was formerly employed at Mews. McDowell's motor works, Barbauld-street. He was educated at Beamont Council Schools. He attended Orford Sunday School. lie was a member of the Ist Warrington Troop of Boy Scouts.

He subsequently =Lased away on May 4th last at Luttrich, Germany. Private Thomas, who was 19 years of age, was tho son of Mr. J. H. Thomas, an old Ws:ring- tonian, who emigrated to Canada two writ ago, being previously employed by the Maypole- Dairy Company, Market Gate: Be joined the colours upon the call of the Motherland, and after a short period of train- nag in Canada he came over with the bet Canadian sontingent, who, after having the nishing touches put to them on Sali*ply Plain, were drafted Over to Fkanee in jry Zak.

IS IT NOTHING TO YOU?

"Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass ley?"' —Lamentations i. 6.

" Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country's at war? Is it nothing to you, the thoughtless and gay— Though vengeance is God's which He will repay-- That Duty and Justice both call you to-slay, Now that your Country's at war?

" Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country's at war? Is it nothing to you, all ye maidens fair. Withholding your "boys" from 'doing their share? 'Tis you, yes, you who should bid them—de or dare! Now that your Country's at war.

" Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country's at war? Is it nothing to you, all ye at your ease, That our Empire's sons from far Over-Seas Are forcing Britannia's foes to their knees, Now that your Country's at war?

" Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country' l at war? Is it nothing to you, the shifty lurker, Nothing to you, the slacker and shirker, Nothing to you, the intemperate worker, Now that your Country's at war?

"is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country's at war? Is is nothing to you, ye that preach and bray, Like asses, of Peace, while the Germans pray— " God punish England," as they drink to-- "The Day"; Now that your Country's at war?

" Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by," Now that your Country's at war? Is it nothing to you the damnable yell Of the raping Huns whose lecherous smell Stinks more in the nostrils than fumes frets Hell! New that your Country's at warp

Rs OSSULSTON-RICHE.

Our old and valued friend, Mr. Ossulston-Riclus has turned his forceful pen towards the great task we all have in hand—the ending if the war. There have been many appeals in verse to the manhood of the country, and we think "Js it nothing to you?' will rank high amongst them, calculated as it is to reach all sorts and conditions of men. Mr. Ossulston-Riche has recently met with a run of misfortune. A street accident injured his foot, and now he is in hospital suffering from pneumonia. That his spirit has remained immune the above verses cm-- elusively prove, and we shall all hope that his physical fitness will soon have Ought up once again wifla 104 nteatp.l a.t.ness, A.

A WIFE'S DILEMMA.

In the Manehester City Police Court on Wednesday,43efore Messrs. R. A. Armitage (in. the chair), 4. Allison and George Howarth, the unusual ease in which the identity of Ser- geant Herbert Dandy is in question, was again brought up. • . As reported 'in last week's ": Examiner," Mrs. Herbert Dandy, a soldier's wife, of 227, Clowes-street, West Gorton, Manchester, was notified on July 26th that her husband had been missing since an engagement in the Dar- danelles on July 15th; and shortly afterwards the list was also published with his name ,in , - as missing. 'The next day a man called say- ing he was Sergeant Dandy, ftild lived with Mrs. Dandy for a week—when doubts arose as 'to his identity, and he was apprehended.. The real Sergeant Dandy is a Lymm man, being the son of Mr. and Mrs.. Frederick Dandy, of _liewfields. He -was born :in .Lymm,, but as -a young-man left With the family for 'America,, where they spent a number of years, after- wards returning to the township. Sergeant Herbert Moston Dandy is 33 years. of age. When eight he went with his parents ta America, returning to Lymm three years later. After a year at the village school he became an apprentice fustian-cutter with the late Mr. F. 0. Moston, for three years; thence, ta Messrs. Moorhouse, ginger beer makers,' Manchester; the Ashton Militia; and back eventually to Lymm on his , former work. Next he, joined the local Volunteers under Sergeant-Instructor Howarth, later also enlisting at Preston in the 2nd Loyal North Lancs. On the South African War outbreak he was Malta, Where he offered 14s services in the Malta Mounted Infantry. During this he gained the Distinguished Service Medal by helping to save a conVoy. Only seven officers and,one or two men survived this, the officers getting V.C.'s and the men D.S.O.'s. Private Dandy simultaneously was also promoted Sergeant. He now has another brother in the South as driver in the Army Service Corps, who visited Lymm only last week. At his first appearance in Court the accused Man who, under the name of George Parkin Hall has now been committed to the Assizes for trial) was charged with having falsely repre-, F:ented himself to be Sergeant Dandy. A straightforriard Question.

The Clerk: From the time prisoner first came to your house until the time Inspector Thomas arrested him at Exchange Station had you any doubt he was not your husband? Witness: Ali, yes, I had a doubt, and I sent for my brother, who came and said it was my husband. I doubted him, but my relations all said it was my husband. I have had letters from brothers and sisters saying I ought to be ashamed of myself for thinking it was not my husband. The letters made 'Me .raere7cpriiiiiced it was my husband. I sent for my brother on July 23 because I had some doubt about the man, because we'had been to Whitworth-street, and they told a man from the Ardwicks, who was with us, that there was nothing the matter with him (the prisoner), that he had not been shot, that he had not been a patient there, and that there was nothing the matter with him. _ .

Or suspicions Were then aroused, and I wired for my brother. My brother came the same evening. Prisoner was going to 'throw my brother out. He thought he was a detective. My brother said the man featured my husband 60 much that it was he. He was altered by the shook. She said it was Herbert

===== 0002601_19150821_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
"BACK TO ERIN."

I'vlore About Irish Harvast3rs' Reglstration

WOMEN TO THE RESCUE.

The sudden dislocation of labour in the agri- cultural districts, notably the Lymm locality of Cheshire—to which we referred fully last week as due to the Irish farm labourer's home- ward race to escape registration on account of some obscure fears—has raised the question as to whether women can efficiently take their places.' Many farmers were at first of the opinion that such was impracticable, but in Lymm at least the women are proving of some assistance this time when the position regard- ing the harvesting of corn, potato and fruit crops is so serious. As it is, the loss must inevitably be very great in all affected dis- tricts. "

===== 0002601_19150821_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
Hon. J. E. Cross Interviewed.

' A statement to the effect that women were of nq use in harvesting operations was strongly repudiated in a Press interview with the Hon. J. E. Cross, hon. secretary of the Cheshire County Committee for Promoting the Employ- ment of Women in Agriculture during the war. "It does not appear to be generally known," he -;said, that there are two ',committees for Lancashire and Cheshire working in conjunc- tion with the Board of Trade for the preparing of women for agricultural work in the present crisis. In _answer to the appeal of the Presi- dent of the Board of. Trade some time ago, large numbers of women have volunteered for war service work of various kinds, and many of these desire to take up agriculture. Large numbers have had previous experience, and arrangements have been made by the com- mittees to ,train others at...the County Council- agricultural colleges in both counties. The training comprises' instruction in milking, dairy work, light, farm work, and _gardening. There is therefore a supply of. trained women workers, in addition to those who have been brought up, in agricultural districts." It was to be hoped, he continued, that farmers ,would take advantage of the' women workers who were available. It was mentioned that these workers could be obtained on application at any Labour Exchange in Lanca- shire or Cheshire. There was no desire on the part of the women to supplant men per- manently. They had offered their services dur- ing the war only to help in the national emergency, and the question of wages would have to be settled between the employers and the employes. The joint secretaries for the Lancashire Com- mittee are .Mr. Windham E. Hale, of Preston; and Miss Knowles, of the Labour Exchanges Divisional Offices, Warrington, The Position In Lymm.

This week "The Examiner" representative had an interview with Mr. Wm. H. Winstan- ley, of the Dairy Farm, Rush Green. Mr. Winstanley is the local farmer who has always employed a number of Irishmen during the season, and who will consequently suffer most on account of the flight. Our interviewer found him busily engaged in the fields with his men. " These men- suddenly leaving," said Mr. Winstanley, "throws me into .much difficulty; for practically . speaking they cannot be re- placed without interfering with war service— which I should be thp last to do. •It therefore means extra work for my teamsmen and my- self if the harvest is, to be cut in time before it is dead ' ripe: and there is sure to be a lot of corn shed before it is cut. Whether we shall lose heavily or not depends on how much we can do in the time.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
Helped By The Girls.

"So we have 'carried on' by getting some very good girls who live close at hand and were willing to come and,• help whenever we wanted them—in fact ,I am letting some come this week to see if they can help us with the corn. I find that women can manage to pick and dig, but of course I have not tried them- in harvest at all. .1 have had girls and two boys picking up the potatoes all this season. If our hopes are realised I think We shall manage all right, for I have three very good men, and my father comes and helps me; so we can get the ` par- ticular ' work done.

• "It is the labour jobs that we cannot get done. There is no one to do all the hard rough work. I have 84 acres of wheat and 45 nergs of oats to be attended to; and there are four men besides myself to do it! Of course we cannot get through it unless the weather keeps good for a long time; because when y. 'u have done a hard day's work you cannot do any more —besides, the morning and evening dews pre- vent long cutting-hours. All that wet weather, 4,150, has knocked the -:)ra iown badly." . ,

On The Bench.—At the Lymm Petty Sessions held on 'Wednesday morning, there were on the Bench Messrs. A. Watkin (in the chair), W. Peter Rylands, S. W. Gould, H. Holden and IL H. Gotobed, and the Hon. J. E. Cross. Mr. T. J. Ridgway appeared, but as a witness.

Mr. K. N. Tweedale.—We are glad to hear that Mr. 1.. N. Tweedale, of Rose Bank—the chairman of the Lymm Red Cross Working Men's Committee—is now recovering from his recent illness, and hopes presently to resume his public duties.

High Legh Resident's Death.—We record with regret the death Of Mr. Richard Shakeshaft— an old High Legh•resident and a wheelwright and lariner—which sad event took place at his residence, Swineyard-lane, on Saturday, after an illness lasting some time. The deceased gentleman's age was about 76.

Freak Potato.-11r. J. IL Wareham, fish and game dealer, of Eagle Brow, is in possession of a freak potato which looks for all the world like a "teddy-bear." This was grown by Mr. Thomas Doan, farmer, of -Spring-lane, Heatley, and it was given by a friend to Mr. Wareham, who is having it photographed.

Mr. William Rigby.—One of the Territorials at the Front, in the course of a letter home, says he saw in the paper about tho young gentleman from 'Mr. Evans' winning in some of his profession," and appreciates it. He refers to the successes of .21r. William Rigby, of West Houghton, which we reported a fort- night ago.

Death Of Mr. Alfred Percival.—We regret to record the death of Mr. Alfred Percival; of 2, Moston-street, who died on Wednesday after- noon at the age of '4B. Mr. Percival was single, and was employed as a window-cleaner with Mr. Wm. Moston. lie leaves a mother (in Macclesfield), four brothers (two of whom are serving at the Front) and three sisters. The funeral takes • place at the Parish Church on Monday affernoon.

Lighting.—At Wednesday's Sessions Percy. Warburton, farmer, Mersey House, Rixton, came up for driving a motor cycle and sidecar without a red light at the rear.—P.C. Bolsover said that at 10.20 on July 18th he noticed in Mill-lane, Heatley, that Warburton's light iirds missing. He blew his whistle, and Warburton stopped; and he asked him to relight his lamp, which it took him ten minutes to recharge.— Tie fine was as., including costs.

Hom e From Dardanelles.—Lance-Corporal Reg. Hillier, of the Bth Manchesters, arrived on Tuesday home at the Cross, Lymm, for a few days' leave. He was wounded some weeks ago. in the leg in the Dardanelles, and after sojourning for a time in hospital at Malta, came to Netley and thence to Winchester. He is still a little lame, but makes good progress and ex- pects shortly to rejoin his regiment. With characteristic modesty he declined to submit himself to the tender mercies of our inter- viewer.

Tribute to High Legh Roadman.—At Wednes- day's meeting of the Buck-low 'Rural Council, the Hon. J. B. Cross paid a striking tribute to the memory of a roadman, Whose death was an- nounced. Mr. Cross said Rdllinson was'one of the old-fashioned type of servants, a race that was fast dying out. ,He felt that he had lost an old friend. He was a perfectly stiaightforward and honest old man, and a thorouglr.gentlemari in, every -respect.---Thomas Rollinson,, of High Legli, had been in the employ of the Council and its predecessor for nearly 'fifty years, retiring two months ago.

From Heoge.—A local gentleman just re- turned from holidays says that on the journey home he travelled with a jovial soldier who took part in the heavy fighting quite recently at Hooge, and helped to capture three lines of German trenches. He had been out there since September, and Was glad to have a few days away from the 'Jack Johnsons,' which made in the ground holes large enough to contain railway carriages. He had also a German officer's revolver which he had found on the battlefield, andY a case of German ambulance scissors,; At the prospect of returning to the Front he did not appear' downhe,arted.

Not \rery Nice.—Private Henry Cadman, of the sth Cheshire,s attached Royal Engineers. in a letter received by his step-mother at Booth's Hill on Thursday, says that there- is an ex- tremely bad shortage of food, and goes on that rats are plentiful, and " a.s big as cats." It is very amusing to watch them during the day as they fight in the soldiers' dug-outs; whilst at night as the men are digging they run round,,and:,between the legs. _O. the. ,men.!, Writing -previously he said: "-We have been up and down the country this last fortnight," during which,he has not seen the other Lymm " lads."

"lifirst Dangerous", drinking Practice.—.To Wednesday's Petty •Sessions Eli Spence, market gardener; of Thinharn • Massey ;(who did not appear) was summoned, for-M& having had control of ..his horse at Warburton.—P.C. Prominent Heatley Bowlers.—By beating Lancashire on Saturday, Cheshire won ,the county championship of the North of England bowling world. Few matches have attracted such attention—on account of the fact that each side had played three games and was still unbeaten, victory either way meaning the championship. Large crowds assembled to see the match, which was played in two sections at Bowdon Vale and Rainhill. The play at the Cheshire ,centre proved wonderfully keen; and, though Lancashire put up a splendid fight, Cheshire won by two points on the last game: At Rainhill Cheshire did rather better, despite a first .strong lead on, the part ,of their opponent; and on the two sections gained the championship—a result not altogether un- expected. ' It reflects credit on Ileatley that included in the Cheshire team 'which' did so well at Rainhill were two residents—J. Smith and A.. E. Davies. Of the total score of 227 each , Ifeatleyite gained 21—performances equalled only by two of their colleagues.

A CALL FROM THE .TREI'kHES.'

At night, when I think of the trenches, The Lymm lads, I seem to hear say, "Well, Jack, we've left plenty behind us That ought to be fighting to-day."

Just fancy you see them all fighting In ruddy and blood-sodden fields. Perhaps where your pals have been slaughtered By the foe's murderous fire and steel.

So lads, just make it your duty To stand by your pals, thick or thin, What an honour 'twould be for us Lymm folkg To hear you'd got into Berlin.

Some fellows, no doubt when they read this, Will smile, yea, and laugh it to scorn. But, remember lads it's your last chance, Remeraber that little Mite form.

W,-,BROADFIELD;

"EXAMINER " SPECIAL.

A serious, problem has for several months been arising in Lymm. It is in bonnection with the "trippers," many of whom, arrive in motor chars-a-bane and coaches, and indulge in all sorts of practices—therefore not enhanc- ing the fair reputation of the village; and yet who cannot be "done without" because a large proportion of the population rely for a living mainly upon them, by virtue of the money which they bring to and spend in the town- ship. The case is rendered still more difficult because in many respects the hands of the police are tied; they cannot bring to Court all the /cases of 'conduct which are generally con- sidered displeasing, but which yet are not sufficiently improper to justify a charge.

SHOCKING SCENES.

Only a fortnight ago we published from a Lymm resident a letter describing a typical instance—August Bank Holiday. The writer said:—

"I believe in all men, women and children enjoying themselves to their utmost content and happiness, in a right and legitimate manner. But what happened on Monday? Nine out of ten women were much the worse for intoxi- cating drink. A number were not capable of looking after themselves, while some were shockingly indecent, many respectable Lymm people being ashamed to pass through their own*village. What shall we say- when it comes to a woman turning somersaults? I consider that Monday's (*graceful scenes Will be a great lesson to the young men and women of Lymm who saw these women under the influence of drink and acting in such an unseemly and indecent manner. You will notice I do not y anything of the men. There were very few men with the women, and they were less inebriated than the females. .. . Perhaps even these women have husbands and sons giving their life's blood for their (these women's) safety."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DRUNKENNESS TROUBLE.

At a special Court on Tuesday morning, be- fore Messrs. S. Davies and R. H. Gotobed, Mary Jane Strafford, of 3, West-street, Clayton, Manchester, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly at Lymm the previous afternoon.—Sergeant Davies said that with about twenty other trippers she was brawling and making a tremendous noise; she was drunk. She was asked to be quiet, and went on a few yards, but then made use of bad language.— The Chairman, in fining the woman 10s., said this conduct would have to be stopped. Trippers were welcome to Lymm, but they must behave themselves.—Mr. Gotobed associated himself with those remarks.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCIL'S PREVIOUS PROTEST.

Doubtless it will be remembered that last year, under the chairmanship of Councillor Warburton, much discussion took place in the Urban District Council anent the " beery " visitors, and the-result was the dispatch of the following letter to the Chief Constable:—

" I am directed by my Council to Call your attention to a matter which has given considerable annoyance in this village dur- ing the last few years. "In the summer holiday season a great number of visitors come to this district from the neighbouring towns and some of these people misbehave in a very unpleasant man- ner, and half-intoxicated women in par- ticular,. cause a considerable, disturbance in the place. • . "In writing to you . my .Council do not wish to cast any aspersion on the local police , adrainistration, which is very efficient, nor do my Council blame the local hotel proprietors in any way. If you can do anything to mitigate the . existing evil either by additional assistance for the police or by some other methods, I can assure you my Council will be very grate- ful. "Trusting you will give this matter your -kind and early attention, "Your obedient servant, "W. IlfuLLAnn." This letter was , duly acknowledged, with the promise that the matter should receive atten- tion. Later it was reported that the trouble had been considerably mitigated. Councillor Cowper was one of the prime movers in the remedy of this matter—he himself had suffered, as much as anybody from it, for a four-in-hand drew up almost opposite his house, and it was suggested that they did it as an annoyance to him for bringing this matter forward; they had a barrel of beer, and were " dishing " it out opposite his house—and the very satis- factory. result of the Council's activity was hugely due • to' Councillor Cowper. 'Unfortun- ately that satisfaction has proved only short- lived, for the trouble is brewing once again as badly as ever.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OBSCENE LANGUAGE TROUBLE.

But though—as our correspondent has pointed out—the complaint of excessive drinking can- not be laid against the men-trippers, there are other offences which, at least in times of peace, would be hardly less bad. One of these is the use of obscene language, in. which respect local Police Court -cases haVo mcurredwithSad frequency.

At the_ Petty Sess-sions held on Wednesday, William Snake, labourer, of 48, ,Kirk-street, Ancoats,Manehester, :pleaded -guilty to, and *as fined ss. for, the use of obscene language.to the annoyance -of passengers in Mill-lane, Heatley, on July- I.7th.—P.C. Bolgover proved the case.— Defendant said he was sorry, but could. not remember what he said. His son had come home from the Dardanelles, and he (the father) had a drop of drink to celebrate it when he came to Lymm. It was accidentally done, he supposed, as it was not a:practice of his to use bad language.

Superintendent Sutherland: I may say that this conduct is becoming an annoyance in Lymm:

The Chairman (Mr. A. Watkin) then saiel to defendant: Well, you came to Lymm, and we don't want this sort of people. I know there is a great deal of this being done, and we mean to put a stop to it at once. , People get good wages, and they spend them on drink while their brothers are fighting for us away on the Continent! THE GAMBLING TROUBLE

But even this is not the worst, for the gamb- ling trouble also shows signs of alarming growth amongst the Lymni men-trippers despite the exertions of the police; and here again we have been -provided with an example only this week.

At 'Wednesday's Sessions, Squire Shering,ton and George Davidson, colliers—respectively of 2 and 6, Longton-street, Hindley—were charged with having gamed with cards in Paddock-lane, Warburton, at 4.15 p.m. on July- 18th—namely, by, playing banker and passing money. TO the latter clause of the charge- they pleaded not guilty..

It was stated that in consequence of a com- plaint Sergeant Davies and a constable pro- ceeded to the spot, but on their approach the six men got up and ran away, the police having seen them putting money on the cards. Each officer, however, succeeded in capturing one Of the men and one was asked for the cards. He declared persistently that he had none, but they were got out of his pocket. Both abso- lutely refused to tell the names of the other four or to aid the police in any way. Defendants said the other four were strangers to them; whilst Sherington caused some laughter by explaining that "you need not play banker for money; you can play it for ' love 'as they call it." However, each was fined ss. including costs. RESIDENTS' RESPONSIBILITY.

The utmost possible vigilance on the part of the police, who in these days lead a particu- larly arduous life even without all these extra troubles—cannot obliterate such tendencies in a district so scattered and (in many parts) con- cealed as Lymm. They can only 13e abolished by a unanimous, concerted and determined personal action on tho part of local residents ,therrelves.

LYMIVI TERRITORIALS.

"U IN THE LINE AGAIN, BLT NOT IN THE TRENCHES."

Writing on Tuesday to Miss •BeS,sie Cross, of Bridgewater-street, Private Percy Clare—of Booth's Hill—says: "Well, we are up in the line again, but not in the trenches. We are on outpost duty... . We are in a small village, and although we are so near the enemy there are a few people living here. It is nice and quiet up to now, and they have not shelled the village yet. We are living in empty houses, and for beds we have wire-netting put up on woods —almost like spring beds. We have so many hours on duty and so many hours off in the daytime; we are on guard. . . And at night some go on guard on the riverside, and others go on patrol walking round the swamp, and across it in parts. Meet Germans In Swamp.

"It is not so bad as trenches as long as they don't shell the village; but when we go out at night we have to chance meeting the German men on the same game; but we always stand as geod a chance as them if our men meet them. "This village puts me in mind of our dear little village at home, and the water—it is something like being on the lakeside at home; only we are on a different job over here. . . I hope to see 'you once again before long, as they have not 'stopped our leave yet.; you know it takes a long time, as one, out of each company. goes every week. It might -come our turn -be- fore we expect it with a bit of luck. 4, "We are having some very nice weather here, though it is raining a litle now. You say it is twelve months since I left home, but I don't think we shall be away another twelve. We have heard this morning that the Dar- danelles have been forced. I only hope it is true!"

===== 0002601_19150821_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
Out In Open, Holding Village.

Private Percy Clare, in a graphic letter. on Wednesday, the 11th, to Mrs. G. Davies, Booth's Hill,. says: "We have landed at last. We are at a nice quiet spot—not in the trenches; we are out in the open holding a village. .. . We are, not far from them. .. . We work it in reliefil, so we get a good rest when .we are off duty. We live- in empty houses, and have the best beds that we have had since we have been out here. .. . We don't mind how long we stay here! They have not shelled the village yet; you would be sur- prised to see people living in the village so near. "We have some trenches just behind us on a hill, in a 'big wood, and some of our battalion are in them. But our company are in front of the trenches in the little village; and between the Germans and us there are two or three rivers and a big swamp—all water, and marshy all round the village. And it is our duty to guard all round the swamp. Some are on guard round the water; and some go out at night on what we call patrol, looking for the enemy. Up to now I have been on gijard on the water side. just by the side of a wood. It is a bit lonely at night. only it makes it all right with two being on duty at night—and we have gof a bit used to being in lonely places by now.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans Wound Little Lad.

"But we have to keep our eyes open, you know, as we never know what sort of, a game they try on sometimes. They sent a few shells over yesterday, but they missed the village and went in the wood on the hill. One lad was walking about in the wood, and a little bit scratched him on the chest; it Was not much. "I expect we shall take it in turns—so long in the trendies in the wood, and so long on the job we are on now. It is all right ,in the wood—plenty of good dug-outs if they shell; so we shall be all right. There is one thing: There are no bullets flying about, and the only thing we have to do is to keep a good look out for fear they might sneak up to us at night. . . "We have got W. Madden with us again; he came last night. . . . The boys are in the best of health at present." Corporal Jack 11. Moston writes: "We are going on very nicely, and having lovely, weather. It keeps very hot. . . . I shall be coming to see you some day with a bit of luck.:

Priiate James Taylor (first Lymm "Terriee wounded), writing from the 4th Northein General Hospital, Lincoln, to his mother at I, West Hyde, says: " I am in the best of health. I am now in the old place as you will see by the address) and the arm doesn't seem to be getting any better. But it will take time, as the doctors say. So I am expecting my dis- charge from the Army altogether, but expect it will be a good time yet. Sorry to hear about Percy coming home with the accident."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans In Opposite Village.

Private Henry Brookes, of 1, West Hyde, in word to his aunt, Mrs. Driver, of Mill-row, The Grove, says: "Since I came baCk to the regiment after having mT furlough we have moved to another position along the line-=-sorhe aistanco of more than a hundred miles' ,away from our last place. We are not in the trenches here; it is a different kind of warfare altogether.

All our work is done in the open with a swamp and a river dividing us from the Germans. You see we are occupying a village on our side, and they have another small village on their side_ Things have been very quiet with us since we came here, and it is a fine job, con- sidering we have always, up to coming here, been in the thickest of the fighting. It is a fine country down here, and there is a tre- mendous let of fruit—peaches and apricots and grapes galore growing outside! "I only wish it was all over, and we were back at home. But for myself I cannot see any prospects of it finishing yet."

The Private's home is at Reddish -View, Rush Green, but lie is at pres- ent in St. George's Hospital. London, having had a nervous breakdown in France. The Ser- geant-Major found his son making good progress towards 'recovery, though still very ill.

DISTURBED COMMITTEE

Educationists Give Evidence In Cruelty

Case.

George Walker, carter, of Higher-lane, Lymm, appeared at Wednesday's Sessions on a sum- mons for having, on Friday, July 30th, cruelly ill-treated a horse.—He pleaded guilty, but added that it was done through provocation, and not wilfully. Mr. T. J. Ridgway (chairman of the Lymm Administrative Sub-committee) stated that a fortnight ago he was at a meeting at the Coun- cil Offices, when in consequence of what was going on outside they all jumped up and saw a horse on the floor. Directly it got up Walker commenced to kick it in the ribs. He con- sidered it deliberate cruelty. Mr. W. Mullard (clerk to the Education Com- mittee) said that on the day in question—at five O'clock in the evening—he was looking through one of the windows of the Council Offices and saw a cart loaded with stone. He saw the horse fall down, and when it got up the driver kicked it (he should say) six times on the right side. They rapped on the window and went out to him. He was wearing clogs with sharp iron tips, and was using a stick at the same time.

Sergeant Davies, of the Lymm police, said that in consequence of a complaint rhe saw defendant, who said the horse was carting stones from the railway station, and at the top of the kill it slipped and went down; after it got loose he hit it with the aide of his foot to liven it up—he did not hurt it. Defendant told the Bench that he had been driving "all his life," and had never been up in Court before. If anyone went to see the horse he thought they would find nothing wrong with it.

In reply to a question, defendant said he worked for himself. He also called a witness— his brother.

Harold Walker, of Sandy-lane (the witness in question) said the horse had been very rest- less during the day, but whai was done at the time was not done in regard to cruelty—he was there all the time. His brother was with the horse when it went down. There-Were two
