XI HO RESCUED THE CHILD?

Sir,—l notice in yesterday's "Examiner," regarding the fire which occurred in Scotland- road some time ago, that several men (who were working on the new building next door) are to be recommended to the Humane Society for meritorious conduct. "NOT PAINTERS' WORK."

Sir,—ln your paper of Saturday last I notice a report under the heading "Not Painters' Work."

• • Your correspondent seems to have been in a humorous mood when he speaks of painting with whitewash.

The report is too ridiculous to take seriously, hut my society would esteem it a great favour if you will grant space for the true facts, which are as follows:

There are about a score of labourers em- ployed in painting the gas-holders and other iron-work at Longford Gas Works, and my society claim that this work should be done by qualified men. I wrote to Mr. Haddock to this effect, hut his answer was—it was only labourers' work.

I think the cleaning and protection of iron- work is very important work and cannot be done justice to by the ordinary labourers, and I venture to say the average ratepayers will agree with me. My society asked Mr. Bobbing- ton,, our delegate, to bring the matter before the Trades and Labour Council, with a view to bringing the Same before the Gas Com- mittee.

THOS. JONES, Secretary N.A.S. House and Ship Painters. 28, Algernon-street, Warrington, August 16th, 1915.

PRIVATE JOSEPH DANIELS.

Lymm Tanner Wounded In The Dardanelles.

Mrs. Daniels, of The Rookery, Booth's Hill, received .on Thursday night official notice that her husband, Private Joseph Daniels, of the South Lancashires, had been wounded in the Dardanelles. No details were given. The Private has been writing home quite regu- larly, but not recently—evidently not since his wounding. Private Daniels, who has two little children, is employed at the Walker's Tanyard, Grap- penhall. Previously he worked as a labourer for Mr. John H. Hoc, of Booth's Hill. He attends the Parish Church. He enlisted after the war outbreak, and left this country somt few months 4go.

VOLUNTEERS TO BE USED.

Sir Arthur Haworth's Significant Visit To Lymni.

Sir Arthur Haworth and his adjutant, Captain Milne, of Altrincham, paid a visit on Wednesday night to Lymm, and inspected the local platoon of the Cheshire Volunteers at half-past eight at the Drill Hall. Captain G. Williamson and Sergeants S. D. Moston and _J. Lister-Farrar were in charge.

After walking down the ranks, Sir Arthur watched the men go through drill, afterwards addressing them. In his address Sir Arthur referred to the efficiency of/the whole battalion. and mentioned that there were now several sets of entrenching tools at their disposal. Of thes, he hoped the men would make good use. With reference to the speech made at Chester on Bank Holiday by Sir ()More Creagh, V.C., G.C.8., military advisor for the whole Volun- teer movement of the country, he said they could take it for granted that they would be used for active military duties before long— either in relieving men in garrison towns, or in doing patrol and sentry duty in the various alien camps in the county.

Wounded Warriors' Outing.—On Thursday afternoon the wounded soldiers from the Red Cross Hospital, together with Lance-Corporal Hillier and Lieutenant Thompson (who have been respectively N.vounded and invalided home), were entertained at the invitation of Mrs. Saville and Mrs. Gotobed. One of the sisters and a muse were also in attendance. The out- ing was held on tiLe church side of the lake, ancl tea was arva. The day was most pleaBantly spent in fishing competitions, for which prizes - were offered—for the (1) *malleet and (2) the fast fish (Won by Lance-Corporal Hillier), for (3) the biggest (won by Private Craggs), and (4) for the next largest (won by Private Price). Before the return home a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the hostesses, and cheers given for the hospital and• Army.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAPPENHALL.

Tanyard Trip.—Laat Saturday Walker's Tan- yard, Grappenhall, had their annual trip to New Brighton. Most of the tanners' wives accompanied them, journeying from Warring- ton by the ten o'clock ,train. The weather in the morning was showery, but turned out some- what better in the afternoon. Most of the day was spent in the Tower Grounds and on the sands, all being well pleased with the outing.

Horse Bolted.—On Tuesday great excitement was caused in Grappenhall by a runaway horse belonging to Messrs. Strange and Sons, bakers, Warrington. It appears the man in charge. accompanied by a boy, was delivering bread in Knutsford-road.. near Forrest-street, when the straps supporting the shafts broke, causing the van to fall near the horse's legs. The horse took fright and bolted at full speed over the Swing Bridge. It was stopped near Leigh-villas, but was badly injured on the fore leg. Luckily, no one was hurt, but some children near th° Swing Bridge had a narrow escape.

PENKETH FLOWER SHOW.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
An Effort ln Aid Of Stricken Belgians.

WAR-TiiVrE CULTIVATION.

The twenty-second annual show of the Pen- keth, Sankey and District Garden and Cage Bird Society was held in the Sankey Park on Sa.turday. There was a good muster when Dr. Murray performed the opening ceremony. In asking him to do so, Mr. William Woods, chair- man of the Society, said that, notwithstanding the inclement weather and the bad times through which they were passing on account of the war, it was with great pleasure that he noted that this, the twenty-second annual show, was being held. He thought that it behoved us as a nation to study economy; all people should be able to grow a few potatoes or cabbages. If the people would only do all they could in this respect it would be beneficial not only to them- selves but to the community at Large. He would like to thank all who had sent flowers, fruit, &c., for sale in.aid of the Belgian Famine Fund.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. MURRAY AND OUR DEBT TO BELGIUM.

Dr. Murray, in declaring the'show open, said he was wondering as be came along how many shows had been held at Penketh, and he found that this was the twenty-second. The show began in a very small way but it had gradually grown, until it had developed into the position in which they saw it that day. They all knew how difficult it had been to grow flowers, &c., this year. For some days now there had been thunder-storms, and altogether more water than was required. He hoped that the people would still keep up their gardening, and so enable them to continue with the, show for a long period to come. - A great deal had hap- pened since the last show was held twelve months ago. ,Optimists and pessimists had been deceived as to the 'course of events, and we still remained at a standstill as to the war on the Continent. We all remembered that the first block to our enemies was a little country called Belgium. Had it not been for them the Germans would have walked right through to France, and our own little island might have been in the same position as Bel- gium is to-day—the property of an enemy. We owed to those people a very great debt--a debt which will be very difficult to. repay. That' afternoon opportunity had been taken of mak- ing use of the show ground to raise funds to assist those Belgians who were yet in their native land, without food and without the means of getting it. There were seven million of the ,still' in their own country. No Eng- lishnian could get into Belgium to tell us whether they were getting the food which was being sent to them, but we had the assurance of the Consids of neutral countries, particu- larly the Americans, that the Germans did not touch any food sent to the Belgians: They at Penketh had done a good deal for the Belgians in many different ways. He would ask them to help once again towards finding food for those who were still destitute. There would be quite a number of different ways of persuading them to part with their coppers, and he hoped they would be a success. (Hear, hear, and applause.) In proposing a hearty vote of thanks to Dr. Murray, Mr. Foulkes said he thought , there was no Other man who had taken such an interest in the work of the show than Dr. Murray. Mr. James Ray seconded the vote of thanks, and it was carried with applause. PRIZE LIST. . GARDENS, &c.

NEW BOOKS.

Taras Bulbat By Nikolai Gaol. London: The Walter Scott publishing Co.. Ltd. is. net.

The interest in all things Russian increases as the stupendous struggle among the nations pro- ceeds. Especially is this the case in literature by Russian authors. A contribution has, just been Made by The Walter Scott Publishing Co., Ltd., in issuing a is. net edition of one of the nlost fascinating of Russian books—" Taras Bnlba," by Nikolai Gogol,: an epic in which the prowess and daring of this terrible Cossack (" kozak " means knight, or warrior) are painted with characteristic, rorcible brilliancy. Strange it is that the Russian classics such as those of Gogol should not make a greater appeal in this country than those of (say) Turgenev or DostoYevsky—who after all, though "realists," are no guides to the true Slavic heart. Gogol is romantic; but on the other hand he is more of a realist. And though he may be apt to jest with a smile rather bitter, he at least gives us a faithful picture of the type of Russian character—which we shall badly need by and by. Apart from political urgencies, however, the books of Gogol are worthy of wide perusal be- cause in times like these their rugged nature and impetuous spirit must appeal to every Briton. And certainly "Tams Bulba " is no less valu- able to the student of Russia than is "Michael Strogoff."

JUDGES INTERVIEWED.

In conversation with the joint secretary—Mr. G. Hooper—the " Examiner " representative learned that this year there were many more entries and members than previously, and that altogether greater, interest had been taken in the movement. Owing to the country's call the Society had a depleted committee, and the few remaining members had worked very hard. But they did not mind that, and certainly the result justified their labours—indeed, if they went on at the present rate the schoolroom would presently not be sufficient to contain the entries. "The number of entries," said Mr. Hooper, "has been a pleasant surprise, and if the support merits it, the committee in- tend to make every effort to increase the prizes next year." Next the "-Examiner " man interviewed the judges—Messrs. T. Harrison, of Greeneld, Thelwall ; and R. Poulton, of Thelwall Heys— who stated that the feature of the show was the Ladies' Section (five exhibits), in which the awards had been carried off, by Miss Fenton, Miss Cheetham and Mrs. Woodcock. Good arrangement and taste was shown. Mr. G. Hooper's collection of perennial hardy flowers was also very good. The mixed flowers and foliage deserved commendation, the first and second of the six entries being exceptionally pleasing considering the weather; in all much taste was shown in the arrangement. Very keen competition in the sweet pea section produced a good class. There was a pleasing result in the class for three vases of flowers (distinct varieties), the first prize winner standing out well. The flower bouquets of the children showed keen contest.

In the collections of vegetables the eight entries were specially well produced, cabbages— including green ones—being ,a strong class. Beans and peas were also good. A tribute was merited by the fine quality of Mr. T. Nixon's non-competitive collection of vegetables, in- cluding tomatoes.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
HOSPITAL HELPED.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 21, 1915.

LATCHFOIID GARDENING.

Highly SLECCe3GfLiI Show Heft! Despite War.

HELPING THRIFT.

Whilst regretting to see some gardens unculti- vated, we are pleased to see a general marked improvement. We trust still greater efforts will be made.

Such is the Sentiment expressed in this year's report of the Latchford Garden Society, and signs that it will be realised were (Aearly given at the third annual summer show held in. the Wash-lane Schools and adjoining park. Although in a Position free from anxiety (the. balance-sheet shows a year's income of .217 19s. 5d., and a balance in hand of over £8), funds are, of course, always welcome for the development of the movement. The committee, which has carried the society .on during the troubled times of war consists of: Messrs: C. Foster (chairman), W. Kemp •(vice- chairman), G. Hooper and J. S.. Burgess (joint hon. secretaries), T. Nixon (tre(tsurer), Rose- bottom, Harrop, Shakeshaft, Drury, Cooper, Wakeford, Howliston, Hadkiss,., Johnson, Tole, Gerrard. T. Taylor, 'Lawless and Flindells. Many of these have joined the colours. ,

===== 0002601_19150821_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
"THOUGH UNDER DIFFICULTIES."

The opening ceremony was performed in the schoolyard by the Mayor (Alderiaan Peacock). The Mayoress also attended, and the Chairman was the Rev. G. C. Briggs (president). Many wounded soldiers from Raddon Court were in- cluded in the assembly—which was unfortun- ately small owing to the weather. The Chairman thought he might say in regard to the present Mayor of Warrington that-of all the holders of that high position of chief inagiS-, trate of this town—though all lent men—not one had excelled' him in Close and conscientious attention to his duties.. if he could encourage any good cause he was willing •to put himself to any amount of trolible ,to do it: ,(Hear-,,hear.) That was what he had done that afternoon, arid- they were-- all thankful to him for it. The Latchford Garden_ Society was at present only, of course, in its infancy; and when the war broke out last year received, like many other things, a considerable shock. Hap- pily it survived that shock, and was now doing well, though under difficulties. They wanted everybody in Latchford to jdih, even,if-they had not a garden; they could still give it their sup- port. They were trying to make Latchford. more beautiful, and at the same time to provide fa:healthy and pleasing Occupation to those who laboured in its interests.' They were also .in- culcating thrift (so vital at present) by encour- aging the people to produce their own vegetables. (Applause.) "OVER THE WATER" AND AT HOME.

The Mayor, in opening the .show, explained that the Mayoress and himself should now have been on holiday amid the Welsh mountains, but knowing how necessary it was during these troublous times that encouragement should be given to the pursuits of peace, they thought they could not do better than come to Warrington for that function. He did not think that any- thing better could be given to the people than some hobby to cultivate a better idea of things; and the success of this society was very gratify- ing to its members. Often he thought what a different feeling they must have from the men over the water—when every night they were attending to their flowers and vegetables, and yet what was going on over there? It was neces- sary to draw their attention away from those things, and they could not have anything better than a gardening society. He supposed they had copied Stockton Heath; and hoped that they would go on improving and become better than Stockton Heath—if . they were not already! (Laughter and applause.) , My. Wm. Tinsley (a vice-president) moved, and Mr. S. Akroyd seconded, a Motion of hearty thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress.. This was enthusiastically carried. Alderman Peacock, in acknowledgment, remarked that doubtless he would enjoy himself as much at the' show as in Wales.

WORKERS AND FiciiiERS.VITH THE WOODS

Sankey Wire [Allis Employes Raise Funds.

SUCCESSFUL BONATLENG MAIN.

Notwithstanding the fact that " the weather last Saturday afternoon Was not of the best from a bowler's point of view, .n.o fewer than, eighty of the emploYes of Mr. W. D. Houghton and Co., Ltd., Sankey Wire Mills, appeared on the Sankey Chapel House Bowling Green to take part in a main, the proceeds of which were to go to the mill benevolent fund. The Contest was patronised by nearly 200 spectators, and the takings, from which £5 will be handed over, may be accounted highly satisfact,ory. The benevolent fund makes regular donations to local charities and provides. extra comforts for works colleagues at the Front, with giants. 'where necessary to the dependents of those colleagues. Mr. George Steeples, the enthusiastic chair- man of the fund, asked the two popular direc- tors of the firm, Messrs. John Hougiliton and Ernest Houghton, amid great applause, to open the main. ,

===== 0002601_19150821_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD RELATIONS BETWEEN MASTERS

AND MEN

•Mr. Ernest Houghton, before the "opening," said he had come to join his workers not as indoster but as a friend and comrade, more espocially during the present crisis. We *ere all fighting for one end and were expected to do our bit for King and country. He thought there ought to exist a better feeling between men and masters; both shotild realise their respective responsibilities. So far as his firm .were concerned no masters had a better feeling ttowards. their men-. Speaking ot the fund he :wished every..man. ta da b.IK best o,furthe,r the ,interests and swell the fund financially as much as he courd affoxd 'and make it A linge success. This 'War meant that every man, weman. an.l child should sacrifice something. " Some men," he continued, " have died heroes' deaths on the battlefield for you and me; therefore why should not we at home make some sacrifice to lessen the suffering*. of their. relatives? One of the objects of the fund is to provide luxuries for those of our men who are now fighting at the Front, and I appeal to you to show your appreciation of their worth by helping the fund as much as possible, so that they will always have in their memory the thought that their comrades at home are think- ing of them, whilst they are fighting our battles. To give you further encouragement I will give to the fund £lO now, and if you will do your proper share, I will increase the amount." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150821_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK WILL WIN.

Mr. John Houghton said he wondered how some of us really reviewed the situation. In this great war we were engaged for our lives, our liberties and prosperity; therefore every man should put forth his fullest possible energy to increase the output of every factory and workshop in the country. "One of the absolute necessities of the pre- sent time," he said, " is work and more work. We cannot all be soldiers, but there is not a man, woman or child but can do something to bring the war to a speedy close. Patriotism does not demand that we should all go fighting, with gun and bayonet, but it does demand that we should do our utmost to win the struggle whether it be in the trenches, on the high seas, in the mill and factory, or wherever we may be employed. This war must go on to the bitter end and until we have absolutely conquered the enemy, and I appeal to every man .here to, go on with hie work at his fullest efficiency. We must win and every atom of strength is needed for the successful accomplishment of the great task before us." _ _

Concluding, he made several references to loyalty of the Colonial troops and their great deeds on the battlefield. Some of his remarks on the war were re- ceived- in absolute silence, and once they brought tears to. the eyes of many men.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPPORT FOR FUNDS.

Mr. Steeples, after tendering the best thanks of the respective committees to Messrs. John and Ernest Houghton for so kindly coming and opening the bowling main, also thanked Mr. Ernest Nought-on, the president of the Benevo- lent Fund for his further handsome donation of £lO. He also referred to a speech made recently by the Mayor of Warrington as regards the donations to lOCal charities during the pre- sent war as compared with the Boer War. He felt sure that when all was taken into account they would find that good work was being done in many directions outside the Local Relief Committee's work. He trusted that every employe of the 'company would generously sub- scribe so that it could never be said that they had not done their duty, not only to the local charities, but also to their fellow men who 11441 left the works to fight the battles in Flanders. Gallipoli and elsewhere.. After the main was finished sixteen valuable prizes, kindly given by -a few gentlemen at a cost of about £7, . were distributed by Mr. J. H. Fleming, and votes of thanks concluded a very pleasant afternoon's gathering.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

Preparing For The Season.

A medting of the Warrington and D;striet Intermediate League was held on Thursday, when several alterations to rules were made. The following rules will be helpful to those who are desirous of joining the League:— Rule 5.—" That all players under the age of 18 on the 30th of April of the current season be eligible to take part in the League . . . (it should. he-noted __that . the . committee' desire, 'birth. certificates to be produced and that alt players will be under military age for the whole of the season). Rule 6.—" That players must be members of the team attached to their old school. The committee; however, may grant transfers on appeal, if sufficient grounds are given for the transfer."

Rule 6A.--'" That any two schools not repre- sented in the League be allowed to combine to form a club to enter the League. Proof- must be given that neither school can raise a team." The membership fee is. 2s. 6d., payable on application for membership. Jerseys and pants are lent .13y the Warrington Club, but clubs may provide their own if they desire.

It is the intention of the committee to arrange a full programme of matches to take place- at WildersPool. So far • the' following clubs have signified their intention of joining the League:— Parochial O.B.; Heathside 0.8., Sacred Heart 0.8., St. Mary's 0.8., St. James' 0.13. The next meeting of the League Committee will be held on the first Thursday in Septem- ber, when fixtures, &c., will be arranged. -Application for membership of the League can be made at once by post to Mr. E. Jackson Mee. hon. see., Mersey View, Penketh, who will he pleased to answer any further inquiries.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
-LEAGUE COMMITTEE'S CLEAR RULINGS.

At a meeting of the Northern Rugby League, held in Manchester on Tuesday, under the presi- dency of Mr. J. H. Smith, of Widnes, a ques- tion was asked as to whether it would be illegal to allow a benefit math to a player during the current season, and the answer was that it would be illegal to allow benefit matches during the period covered by the resolution passed at the last general meeting. It was asked by the Brighouse Club if they could allow players over 18 years of age to take part, in workshop competition matches? The, decision of the committee was that players over 18 years of age could not take part in such matches. •

139 159 PRESENTATION OF CUP AND MEDALS.

The Mayor on Recreation and War Funds. At the conclusion of the final game,

Mr. W. Patrick (hon. secretary) asked the Mayoress to present the cup .and medals, and, int doing 'so, congratulated the employes of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles upon their victory. Alto- gether they had occupied the position they now held on four occasions., (Applause.) It was, how- ever, time that Messrs. Green4ing's carried off the cup., (Laughter.) The Mayoress . then presented the cup to Mr. Spencer on behalf of the winning team, to the other members gold medals, and to the runners- up silver medals. Mr. John Willett (chairman of the committee) said it would be admitted that wider the present circumstances it was hard work to keep the ball rolling, but the committee had done all they could to provide sport for the working men of Warrington. (Applatse.) ' "I have never before," Mr. winat added, "asked anyone to enlist, but I will say that if there is any young man present who thinks' about his country now is the time for him to put up his hand and say 'I will enlist." (Ap- plause.) In conclusion, Mr. Willett proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayoress for her services. The motion was seconded by Mr. F. Dean (vice-chairman of, the committee) and carried.

Replying on behalf of the Mayoress, • the Mayor said they were both most happy when they .were associated with sport where working men congregated. He did not apologise for being present, although a war was taking place, as it was necessary for working men to have recreation to enable them to go through the present strain which was upon them. (Ap- plause.) He was pleased to find that the associa- tion had given £3O. to local war funds. If the. men present had, not gone to the Front to fight with the gallant lads there, they were doing something to render the financial assistance which was.necessary owing to the war. " Why," his Worship inquired, " should we not give of our best, we who are being protected by our gallant lads in the Army, and Navy? If it had not been for them we should have had no England to-day. Therefore, a man who does not de his duty, and feel a pleasure in doing it, and who does not assist the local funds, is not worth the name of Englishman. I congratulate the winners upon their success." (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. Willett, a vote of thanks was accorded to the committee and the hon. secretary.

FOOD PRICES AND WAGES—continued.

over £400,000 per week, or over 3s. per head of those benefiting. These figures are exclusive of increases which have been granted to agricul- tural labourers, seamen, railway servants, police, and Government employes. They also exclude increased earnings owing to overtime.

Less Absenteeism in Mines.

The coal-mining industry during last month contlnued very busy, and the shortage of labour was partly Met by better time-keeping. There was a slight improvement in the weav- ing branch of the cotton trade in July; in the other sections there was little change. A high level of employment was maintained .in the woollen, worsted, and hosiery trades, and there was an improvement in the jute, silk, bleaching, calico printing, dyeing, and finishing trades, and in the plain net section of the lace trade. On the other hand; there was some decline in the linen trade. The boot and shoe trades continued fully employed last month, and there was still great activity in the leather and ready-made clothing trades on Government orders. The bespoke tailoring, dressmaking, and millinery trades showed a seasonal decline: There was a fur- ther falling off in the silk hat trade, but the improvement in the- felt hat trade was main- tained:. The number unemployed in the build- ing trades increased slightly, but was still very low. Th - glass and pottery trades were, on tile whole, rather better employed than in June.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
The Unemployment Figures.

In the 'trades -,compulsorily insured against unemployment, viz., building, works of con-' struction, engineering, shipbuilding, vehicle making, &c., the percentage of unemployment on August 6th was 1.03, as compared with 0.96 a month ago and 3.95 a year ago. These figures relate to the whole of the United Kingdom, , and inelude all unemployed workmen in the insured trades. It will he seen that the rate of unemployment in these trades remains less Ithan a third the rate of a year ago. As regards the uninsured trades, the number of men and women on the -registers of the Labour Exchanges on August 6th shows a de- crease on the figure of a month ago, being 58,618, as compared With 63,693. For. men alone the corresponding figures were 17,274 and 19,993, 'end for women 41,374 and 43,700.

FOCD PRICES AND WAGES.

The Influence Of Twelve Months Of War.

DECREASED UNEMPIAIIMENT.

The Board of Trade "Labour Ga zette " on Tuesday dealt with the fluctuations real food prices in the United Kingdom daring t4c-, thewar,entuted-July 31st, 1913 lt,:",:cit.untittAugust,4th, :1914; the jetonal state:s,` that an_Y inarked general advance, in. prices took place. The articles included are beef and ,mutton (british and imported), bacon, fish, flow, bread, tea, sugar, milk, butter, cheese, eggs aiid potatoes.

Advance In Meat And Fish.

The price of British beef and mutton showed but little advance during the last five months of 1914—less than 10 per cent. on the average. In the early part of 1915 a steady increase occurred, and at the beginning of May the total advance amounted to 20 per cent During May there was a rise of about 14 per, cent., but since then there has not been nauch change. For British beef the present excess of prices over those prevailing immediately befoie the war averages about 40 per cent.; for British mutton it is somewhat less. -

The percentage advance in the prices of im- ported beef and. mutton has been greater than that for the 'home-grown meat throughout the period. For the better cuts of imported meat the advance since the war began is over 40 per cent.; for the inferior cuts 0ve5..60 per cent. From the end of August up to the :beginning of May the average price of baeon Varied be- tween 7 and 11 per cent. above the k.rvel of JUly, 1914. During May there was a rine of about 5 per cent., and .at the end of July the average excess of prices over these or a ye,ar earlier was 18 per cent. The retail price of fish advanced more than about 25 per. cent. on the avdta.ge up to December ISt, 1914, but a series ot increases in prices since that date have resulted in a total ,- average advance of over 60 per cent. as com- pared with a,year ago.

POSITION IN BERLIN.

The Board of Trade "Journal" gives the following/ information as to the peeition Berlin in June :

According to official returns published in the " Statische Korrespondenz," while the prices of several articles of food in Berlin showed a further considerable advance, in Juno note- worthy reductions took place in the prices of others, 60 that on the balance, when allowance is made for the relative importance of the various kinds of food, in working class con- sumption the general level of food- prices showed scarcely any change as compared with May, the rise above the level of July, BM being 65.4 per cent., as compared with 65.0 per cent. in May. In the case of bread end flour the advance in prices, as compared With last year, is partly obscured by changes in quality due 'to various orders of the Federal Oonneil enjoining the use of inferior substitutes or mix- tures instead of. pure wheat or rye flour. Prices. in Vienna in Jnne showed a very large increase except in sugar. Peas went up by 300 per cent. and rice and .beef by over 200 per cent. Pork, lard, bacon, margarine and eggs were all at least twice as dear as in 1614.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

'Do NOT LIVE IN MANSIONS." --- Hatton Rifleman Tells Where The Troops Stay.

Rifleman Percy Cliffe, of the 10th Rifle Bri- gade—whose home address is at Hatton, and who, previous to the war, was employed in Messrs. Crosfield's Printing Department—writes from the Front to Mr. Joseph Makin, of Oldham- street, Latchford "We have just come out of the trenches for a few days, so I thought I would drop you a line. Well, there is not much doing at present round where we are—only sniping. There is plenty of that and the big• guns. There is not rauCh danger if you keep down; the most danger is going in and coming out. We have to go in after dark over fields and ditches, with bullets flying about. But we have only had one rounded, so we can't grumble at that. "The place where we are staying (a little town) is knocked all to piece.. There is hardly a house standing, and the church is completely wrecked. No one would believe it if they had not seen it—all done by the Germans! Well, it is all right if they keep off our little hut. Of course, you know we do not live in mansions on this job; we are in a shippon at present, and we have had worse than this I can assure you!"

===== 0002601_19150821_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
" EXAMINER " IN DARDANELLES.

All Warringtonians Want It At Once.

"'Many thanks," writes Private T. Mannion, a Warrington man, in the Dardanelles, "for your valuable paper. There are a good few from Warrington in this regiment, and nearly everyone wanted to read it at once.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
Waiting To Emulate The " Terriers."

"We have been out here about five weeks, and most of our time has been spent in the trenches. We relieved a regiment that be- longed to the 29th Division who made such a great name at the landing out here. We received our baptism of fire two days after landing, so now we don't jump as ranch when shells are bursting. "We have read how well the ' Terriers ' have fared in France, and we are only waiting for the time to come when the lads in the new Army will acquit themselves the same, and. like the Terriers,' bring credit on the old town.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
An Triers.

"SO far we have been holding trenches, but are shortly expecting to move. ' Our General told us that when the right moment came he hoped we. would tight like rancashire and Englishmen always do. You can bet all the Warrington boys will be triers. "Wishing you every success' for the future."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
About Lenses.

"What do you know about lenses?" was the title of a lecture given to the Warrington. Photographic Society on 'Tuesday evening by Mr. Harold Smith, who presented a dry subject in a most interesting and instructive manner, pointing out the faults and perfections of various lenses familiar to photographers. There was not a large audience, probably due to the fact that the aubjgct could not be called popular and is looked on as 6amew hat abstruse.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Miss Ailsa E. Loekerbie.

We regret to record the death of Miss Ailsa E. Lockerbie, 17 years of age, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Robert Lockerbie, of 124, Chester- road, Warrington. Miss Lockerbie, who was on a visit with her mother and sister at Daresbnry, was taken ill on Monday, and died on Wednes- day. She was a popular member of Holy Trinity Sunday school, and will be greatly missed by many friends. The funeral took place yesterday afternoon at the Warrington Cemetery, and the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton offi- ciated. The chief mourners were: Messrs. R. Lockerbie (father), Walter and Robert Locker- hie (brothers), and Basil Shearer and J. D. Leigh (uncles). Wreaths and flowers were for- warded by the following: "Father, Mother and Daisy," "Walter and Olive," "Robert and Annie," Doris M. Cross, "Gladys and Marie," "Lilian," Elsie Chalmers, "Cousin Dorothy and Connie," E. Gillin, "Eva," "Martha," Willie Dunlop, The Neighbours," "Cousin Nellie and Cousin Joe," Mr. and Mrs. Brereton and family, " Uncle John and Aunt Polly," "Auntie May and family," Mr. and Mt's. A. Titchett, Rev. E. C. E. and Mrs. Carleton, Alderman and Mrs. William Bolton, and War- rington Caledonian Association.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 21, 1915.

SAVE! SAVE! SAVE!

Why Economy Should Be Practised By Rich

And Poor.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO SUPPORT THE GOVERNMENT.

Warrington Thrift Campaign.

A local thriit campaign was inaugurated on Saturday night, when the first of a series of meetings arranged to be held by the Warring- ton Committee took place at Bridge Foot. The meeting was fairly well attended, and some telling speeches were delivered. A second meeting should have been held in Bewsey-stteet on Monday, but owing to the inclemency of the weather it was abandoned. A successful meeting took place near the Cheshire Cheese, Latchford, on Thursday evening.

• Another has been fixed for this evening, at Bridge Foot, time 7.30. Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided at Saturday's meeting, and was supported by Miss Broad- bent, Alderman Tinnion and Messrs. Baker, Geo. Walker and Wilfiid J. Rowland. The Chairman pointed out that the move- ment they were that night supporting was in- augurated in London by the Prime Minister, Mr. Boner Law and Mr. Henderson. A com- mittee had been formed in Warrington, at a town's meeting, under the auspices of the Mayor, and it was hoped there would be a quick response from the people of the town. War had now been waged for -twelve months, and they were all looking forward, to the-next two or three weeks. While we were satisfied, in a way, that the country had held its own so far, we could not look forward with any confidence to a quick issue out of the terrible conflict. It was hardly necessary to impress upon them the consequences of defeat. It would be too appalling to contemplate. They had read of the incursions of the German Army into Belgium and of the terrible suffer- ings which people of all classes had experienced. And if the Germans made a succesful landing in this country, we should suffer similar horrors. And it could only, be avoided by their doing their best to support the carrying on of the war.

everyone, no matter what his position, would have to save something. What that something was to be was for every man and every woman to settle for themselves. They would have to ask themselves whether they were doing right to their country when spending more than was necessary upon food to keep them in health, more than was necessary upon shelter, clpthes or recreation. •

===== 0002601_19150821_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
£3,000,000 Per Day Being Spent.

The war was dependent upon men, munitions and money. They were not concerned that night with recruiting or munitions, but with the pecuniary aspect of the question. We were spending something like £3,000,000 per day on the war.' And to show what an enormous amount that was, if they counted it up as fast as they could, and never stopped for sleep or meals, it would take seven or eight days. The £3,000,0011) a day had to be found somewhere. If the nation spent money without consideration, on enjoyment, the nation would have to borrow from their capital resources, and the burden would fall upon the shoulders of rich and poor alike. The poor would feel it more than the rich, and therefore it behoved everybody to remember that -anything they could do by being more efficient in their work and avoiding unnecessary expenditure, they were putting a nail in the coffin of Prussian militarism. There should be no stinting the weekly wages of the working man, especially those who had only sufficient to meet the necessities of life. No one would suggest that this country would gain by stinting the work- ing man and his family. Workers and Employers Prosperous.

The people of the country were enjoying a considerable measure of financial prosperity. Wages in many directions were high and there was much temptation for rich and poor alike to spend money on unnecessary things. If by checking unnecessary expenditure they could save money and put it into a bank, it would contribute in no small measure to the success of the war. In conclusion, MT. Rylands said the Government had decided to take control of the big companies engaged in supplying munitions of war, and those com- panies numbered many hundreds and would, in the course of a few weeks, number many thousands. The profits they were at liberty to make were provided for, and any profits over and above what was allowed by the Act were made, the whole of the balance would be taken by the. State. There was an idea that employers wee making a fearful lot of money and that the men ought not to be asked to do more than they werei ordinarily doing, but everyone was in the same boat. The employer might be making a little more money that he did before, but the wage earner was also getting more. And every- one was therefore in the some boat and in the same position to do something without looking jealously upon the other.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0271.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN TINNION'S WARNING.

Small Bullets As Effectual As Large Shells.

Alderman Tinnion said he had attended many recruiting meetings to advocate the importance of finding men to fight, but he thought the question of thrift in order to find the silver bullets was quite as important and serious as the provision of men. After the provision of men came the consideration of munitions—the making of high explosi'As and machine guns. We had now got the men, we were getting the munitions, arid we wanted the money that could begot from savings, reducing our imports. and the like. A nation was like an individual. If a man spent more money than he earned—if be spent his capital—his prosperity would not he long. lived. The country was now in the 'posi- tion of the man who was spending more than he was making—we were paying more for muni- tions than we were raising by taxation; we were doing it by means of loans. The money would have to be paid back with interest. .That, in his opinion, could not be done if we went•on in the same way that we were going on before the war.

Organised Germany.

The Germans had very many bad qualities, but at any rate they had this good quality: they were thorough patriots. We had to bear in' mind that the Germans we're theroughly organ- ised as a nation. They believed in their Father- land having been educated for the last forty years to prepare for this terrible struggle. What the Thrift. Campaign Committee were asking the English people to do was to save where they could in order. to-prepare for a rainy day, .and to provide those silver bullets which would help to carry the war to a successful conclusion. The Germans had already done their part in this respect, and done it well. They had already regulated the amount of food each person in Germany was to have. No matter how much money a man had he was allowed to have only so much bread or meat or other foodstuff per member of his family, per week. We did not want the people of this country to be dragooned in that way; but we wanted them to organise voluntarily and to save in every possible way in the firm belief that by so doing they would help to win the war.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0272.txt | included=true | included =====
Why All Must Practise Thrift.

The question of thrift provided the most diffi- cult problem to solve. If they wanted men, or explosives, or guns they• had known factors with which to deal, but the condittons' for thrift were so varied and they had so many ramifications that it\ was difficult to lay iown any hard ,or fast line. People, however, could get the general principle and solve the problem for themselves according to their individual circumstances. It was a difficult matter to ask a man getting 20s. a week or under to save, but it was absolutely necessary that he should save —just as necessary as the man getting 100 s. a week—because when the . war was over work would be sParce and wages would fall off. It was estimated that 75 per cent. of the work 'now being done hr this country was for war pur- poses, either munitions or food and clothing for the armies. When that demand ceased he believed trade would be worse than ever we had known. Was it not essential that all should save something for that rainy day? It was wonderful how the saving habit grew upon one when a start even upon ,the smallest lines had been made. All wanted sufficient food. shelter, clething and reasonable recreation, but

===== 0002601_19150821_art0273.txt | included=true | included =====
Every Mickle Maks A Muckle.

If our population of 45,000,000 saved half-a- crown a week it would mean .t 300,000,000 a year. Everyone could not save 2s. 6d. a week, but while some could save only 6d. others could save 55., and the man who saved the sixpence was doing his share and his duty just as much as the one who could, and did, save more. It was the case of the widow's mite over again—it was all that she had and it was commended above all the rest, though so small. The solution of this question was more than ever in the hands of the women, if they could only see how to avoid extravagance. The time would cone, he believed, when women would not be ashamed to' be _seen in last summer's hat and clothes, irrespective of what was known as "the fashion." Generally speaking, they could save by spending nothing but what was abso- lutely necessary. Large demands for anything increased. prices, so that if they bought more of anything than they' needed, they' would punish themselves by forcing up the cost. Everyone could save something, and a small bullet properly directed might be as effective *as the largest shell. They might not be able to save to provide a silver shell, but they could save to provide a silver bullet. When they had saved a little it should not be put away some- where in a stocking, but in a bank, where it would earn interest for them and help te main- tain the financial stability of the country. In this way they would have done their share. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150821_art0274.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE WAR WAS OVER.

Mr. Baker said it was the duty of everyone to do something in the interests of the country. All were in the same position, rich and o•oor alike. Class distinctions did not count for ,Liuch at the present time, as they had either to sink or to swim together. He was of opinion that when the war was over things would go on better than they had gone before. The most bloodthirsty would have had enough of blood- shed. He failed to see why they could not settle their differences amicably by talk, rather than by the use of extreme measures. (Applause.) This was a suitable time in which to sink all individual opinions, and to follow out what was the obvious national duty. A divided house was no use at all; trade unionists had proved that they could get nothing unless a spirit of unity prevailed. A terrible 'responsibility rested upon the Government, who would be greatly encouraged if they felt they .had the country behind them. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150821_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
BENEFIT TO THE GOVERNMENT.

Mr. Felix Mag,inn inquired how the sayings of the people immediately became useful to the Liovernment?

The Chairman replied that the population of Warrington was estimated at about 75,040, and if, in the coirse of a week, the people exercised economy, they would be able to put by probably from £l,OOO t0...4;2,000. That money went into the savings banks. It would be a credit to those }amiss, which, in their turn, would do something with it. The Government were always wanting money; and floated Treasury Bills. They had to go into the money market, and got banks to advance them money on those bills, but if there were no deposits and no savings, the price of the money went up, and the difficulty of the Government was in -consequence increased. The money put into the Warrington savings banks found its-way to London. Mr. Maginn.: The money finds its 'way to the Government through tho banks, and in a short time :comes back to the manufacturer in the form of orders?

Mr. Rylands, in reply, said that we were com- pelled to buy a lot or things from America, who were busily engaged in preparing munitions for the Government in excess of what we could provide ourselves. Foodstuffs also were sent to us. Our manufacturers were so busy upon home trade for the Government that our exports were very Much less than they were in normal circumstances. Ordinarily our exports were very, much larger than they were, at the pre- sent time, and our imports, because of the needs of the Government, were very great. Exports had to be, paid for by imports, and the result of the imiaorts now being so large and the ex- Ports .so small was that the difference had to be made up in some way. There were only two ways in which that could be done. We had either to send the gold out of the country—and the effect was rather complex, as it depreciated our credit-,or we had to .borrow from abroad, and for all time we should have to pay interest to a foreign country on the debt. That was a burden on all the people of the country, rich and poor alike. It would be a greater advan- tage to the country if the people economised in regard to buying things that came from abroad, rather than in regard to things which were pro- duged at home, because if an article was bought at home it transferred the money or its value from one person in the British Isles to another person. If the purchase was made from abroad the imports, which were already large, were increased, and were far more than we were able to pay for by our exports, and we had, therefore, to do so in some undesirable form. If we bought at home we did less injury to the financial position of the country than if we bought from abroad. (Hear, hear, and applause.) A member of the audience asked if the work- ing classes of the country could be assured that these in better circumstances were doing their best to curtail their luxuries? Mr. Rylands said "Yes." Those in better circumstances were doing their duty, and that wasevidenced by the fact that so many were serving with the Colours.. The spring was the -London season, when the hotels, restaurants and theatres were in full swing. This season, however, two or three theatres only were draw- ing decent houses, while many had been closed altogether, and the hotels and restaurants were also affected. The hotel he stayed at, instead of being absolutely full, was not a third full, and the difference in the state of things which existed could also be seen in the fact that less money was being spent. For instance, the ease of Worth, the swell dressmakers, was a good indication. Rich people were in the habit of making their purchases there, but now Worth's was closed.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0276.txt | included=true | included =====
WHO WILL PAY?

Another questioner asked "If you cannot squeeze the money from the working classes for the war, will it have to be paid for by the moneyed classes? Mr. Rylands said the question raised difficult economic problems. He would put the answer in the following way :—lf. a , person owned .ClOO,OOO of the 'National War Loan, he would, not haveiany money. All he would have ;would he the Government's undertaking to give him' £4,500 a year for what he had lent, but if the country, was ruined none of the money -would be worth anything, and all the most wealthy people could do would be to give of their sav- ings.. The savings of an enormous number of people in small amounts would in the aggregate provide a very substantial sum. If the Govern- ment wanted money they floated a loan from the country. If it was not forthcoming they had to put up the rate of interest, and if that was not enough then it had to he put up still further. •

"It the speaker added, "a great mistake to look at this question in a narrow spirit as though a rich man was in a class by himself and the poor man was in the same condition. They all stood together. If there were no rich people and no prosperity, the working man of the country would starve. If the working men are ptusperous, there must be a certain number of men who are able to be a little better off than

otters, but we, as a community, hang together. I think we must try and keep that in our minds." (Hear, hear, and applause.)

===== 0002601_19150821_art0277.txt | included=true | included =====
11283 EVERY MOMENT.

The Value Of The Small Contribution. Thursday night's meeting, near the Cheshire Cheese, was presided over by Mr. F. W. Monks. There was a fair attendanoe. The Chairman remarked that we had entered into the war with right and proper motives, and, whatever happened, we must do all we could to prosecute it to a successful conclu- sion, and, also to ensure that such a war did not occur again. The Government had bor- rowed the sum of ..E600,000,000. He (the speaker) found it difficult to imagine such a sum. If six hundred million sovereigns were placed edge to edge they would make a gold chain five hundred and ninety-two miles in length. They would make thirty-three rows— edge to edge—between Warrington and Man- chester. There were in this country about 46,000,000 people—men, women and children. If every one of that number contributed a penny a week, it would produce an income of £10,000,000 per year. It would take, on the average, 21d. per person to pay the interest on what the Government had borrowed'. The Chairman concluded by announcing that an- other meeting had been arranged for the fol- lowing Saturday (to-day). The Mayor, he said, was .taking part.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0278.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT WOMEN CAN DO.

Miss Lucy Broadbent then addressed the crowd on the woman's side of the question. She expressed the opinion that one of the most striking features of the war and its effects, so far as we in this country were concerned, was the way in which everybody had done a little to help. It was so easy to say: "Oh, what little 1 could give would make no difference." But that had not been said to any considerable extent in this war; everybody had contributed what little they could- for the comfort of the soldiers and for the relief of distress at home and in Belgium. The women of the country, realising how much was being done for them by their men folk on the Continent, should be the keenest of all in the desire to help in what- ever ways they could. The Government called for economy in meat. We required something, then, if we were to curtail our consumption of meat, that would take the place of meat. We could not live on bread alone. Something else was needed. Miss Broadbent went on to enumerate different articles of food which, could be said to take the place of meat in nutriment. It was not known how long the Government's meat supply would last, and if we could help by eating less meat it would be very valuable assistance.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
Substitutes For Meat.

Cheese, lentils and porridge were amongst the articles of diet Miss Broadbent advocated as substitutes for meat. They contained, she sa.id, the same kind of nourish- ment as was supplied by meat, and many tasty dishes could be manufactured from them with the aid of a smaU recipe book issued officially for the purpose. There was a lot of waste going on in the English household even to-day. It was not SO in France, where the most delicious dishes were prepared and practi- cally no waste went on. If the French house- wife could manage se well, why nat the English housewife? She (the speaker) hoped that one of the results of our present friendship with France would be that we should learn some- thing from them in the way she had indicated. There was a lot of waste of bread .in the English household. The odds and ends of bread which were left over from time to time could be made up into delicious bread puddings, instead of being thrown,away. Another point was that it was more economical to eat brown bread than white, as the, former was more nourishing. Miss Broadbent went on to advocate other economies, 'in regard to butter, bacon and potatoes, advising' the use of a sub- stitute for butter, the consumption of American bacon, which was the cheapest, and upholding the additionally' nutritious properties of the potato when cooked in its skin. She concluded by appealing for economy in gas. One way to effect such economy was to go to bed earlier. As one of the newspapers had it: "Early to bed and early to rise, makes a man healthy and helps the Allies."

===== 0002601_19150821_art0280.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RAINY DAY.

Councillor Wilson said that when we had an abundance of money we were apt to spend it rather recklessly. What we wanted to ao now was to make an extraordinary effort to put every spare penny into the bank. In that way we could help the Government 0 pay for the munitions of war they needed so badly and were now having made in large quantities all over the country. Not only was it helping the country to invest one's money in the way in- dicated, but it was helping oneself, because, when the rainy day came along, all one had to do was to present one's bankbook and say "1 want so much this week." He wanted to make a direct appeal to his fellow working men. He would urge them to cut down all their expenses to the irreducible minimum. Some of the money spent on entertainments, tobacco and beer would .be better invested in the way he had suggested.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE CALLS.

Plinston, who begins on Monday a cam- paign in .Lancashire under the auspices of the Parliamentary War Savings Committee, made an eloquent appeal: In the first place, 'he said, the country called for men; and the response was magnificent. They came up in hundreds of thousands from every village and town in the land and from all parts of our vast Empire. The old blue blood of 'the aristocrat, the red blood of the plebeian, and the green blood of the gal- lant Irishman were all flowing, red together on the stricken Continent, and there was no per- ceptible difference. When the Government was satisfied as to the way the men had come in, and were still coming in, they asked for volunteers for munition making. Again, there was a grati- fying response. Then, thirdly, we were asked for money. The war was costing us, let it be borne in mind, .4:283 every moment of the day— £3,ooo,ooo per day—and although we were 'the richest country in the world, it was, bound to put a strain on our resources. The Individual Conscience. ,

Thero was no class more anxious to do its bit than the working class. It was absolutely necessary for everyone to give up their luxuries, or at any rate, curtail them greatly. This suggestion was thrown out to them all as an urgent necessity; it was left for the individual conscience to decide how to act. A man with a family to rear on 255. a week could not be expected to do anything. It was impossible, and it would not be right to ask him to try and save out of such a wage when there were so many demands on it. The difficulty in such cases was to keep body and soul together. A War For The Young.

The present war was a war for the young, not for the old. It didn't matter so much to the old people how the war ended as it did to the young people who • were looking forward to twenty, thirty •or forty more years. So if a young fellow was. working in a. munition factory and earning a go'od wage, it was for him to save and so provide "silver bullets" to support his comrades in the trenches. The young people were looking forward to spending the best part of their lives under conditions as good as they were before the war—nay, better; and the way to help on this ideal was, for the young fellows either to join the Forces or else work in a munition factory. He (the speaker) did not think there was a nobler cause to fight for to- day than the bringing of the war to a speedy and 'successful eonclusion. Those who could not shoulder the gun nor make munitions, could help by Saving wherever they could.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0282.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERY LITTLE HELPS.

The Chairman, having asked for questions, without any response being made, said that he wanted everyone to bear in mind that they should not say to themselves: "Oh, my five shillings is no good to the Government." Every little counted, and would help to end the war.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0283.txt | included=true | included =====

===== 0002601_19150821_art0285.txt | included=true | included =====
WASTE OF LABOUR.

All Employment Should Now Be Diverted To Useful Channels.

===== 0002601_19150821_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSKIN'S REBUKE OF BISHOP.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—There is an aspect of the -Thrift Cam- paign which is ever and again coming to the surface. Scarcely a day passes but in some prominent newspaper there is a letter from some anxioes Person deprecating the cutting off of certain things when such a course may involve hardship on the maker or vendor of these. The writers say, in effect: "If I stop buying articles I can do without someone will be thrown out of work and perhaps may suffer want." I remember a leading article in no less a paper than the "Daily Mail" claiming bene- fit from the unnecessary expenditure of wealthy people, and expressed in these words:—" It is a further fact that they can only spend their money in two ways—by investing it, when it supplies: industry with capital, its very life blood; or by spending it. In either case they must employ labour." The investment is generally perfectly, legitimate and beneficial, but the wisdom of spending money merely to .employ labour . should • always be challenged. John Ru4kin, in 1879, effectually dealt with this question of giving employment irrespective of what use it was put to, and in reply to a certain Bishop who had argued on the same lines as the "Daily Mail," he wrote:— "I cannot easily .express the astonishment with which I find a man of your Lordship's intelligence taking up the common phrase of giving employment,' as if, indeed, labour were the best gift which the rich would bestow on the, poor. .Of course, every idle vagabond, be he rich or poor, 'gives employment' to some otherwise enough burdened wretch, to provide his dinner and clothes for him; and every vicious vagabond, in the deatructive power, of his vice, gives sorrowful occupation to the ener- gies of resisting and renovating virtue. The idle child who litters its nursery and tears its frock gives employment to the housemaid and sempstress; the idle woman who litters, her drawing-room with trinkets, and is ashamed to be seen twice in the same dress, is, in your Lordship's view, the enlightened supporter of the arts and manufactures of her country." General Good v. Individual Claims. •

This scathing rebuke refers to waste of labour and material in its grossest form; in war-time we must turn the search-light on all waste, and the waste of all. The waste of employment is perhaps the worst of all 1118141. It .is bad in a double sense; it not only pro- duces unnecessary things and consumes useful material, but it absorbs time and energy which might be employed producing necessaries. But some may say: "No doubt this is true, view- ing the community as a whole; economically the reason is logical, bat it does not bring bread and butter to my dressmaker, in whoa I am personally interested, and who maintains herself by making dresses which possibly I and others could do without."

• We must acknowledge that there* is some force in this reasoning, and its appeal gains strength because of the personal association. But is it not a mistake to judge the large question from our own limited outlook, and to .be blind to the large view—l mean the well- being of the whole.? T do not contend that we should ignore the claims that arise out of personal association with individuals, but I •claim that the larger interest should hare the first consideration, and control our general action.

In any 'change someone- must he put to in- convenience and possibly suffering. No inven- tion, no improvement in manufacture, to change in fashion, hut POMO have to endure loss. This cannot be avoided; individuals must always suffer for the good of the whole. There is never a law passed but it hits somebody, but this is not- sufficient mason why better laws should uot be passed.

Work Of 3,000,000 Involved. At the present hour conceiie what would be the result of continuing our old methods of pre-war expenditure, everyone having a surplus spending as before with the object of giving and maintaining employment.: There is little doubt that in the period before the war there were at least. three million persons engaged in em- ployments making and selling articles, or, in giving service, which could be done without. Now we require the energies of all these three million ,workers directed into useful channels, and to the making of necessary things. 13ut this will not be effected if those who use these wares, or engage these services, continue their demand. Labour will only move under the force of circumstances.

The Only Way to End the War. Spending money in ordinary times on unneces- sary things may not be wrong or foolish. It would be a sorry thing if our life were reduced to the bare necessities of life. Pleasure, travel, sport, possession ,of objects of art, beauty and interest; these do much to enrich and brighten life, and without them existence would be dull indeed. We are not mere animals, satisfied if we have enough to eat and a couch to rest on. Our manifold nature demands expansion. Our past expenditure on these extra things has in some cases been extravagant even in normal, times, and our ideas about the effect of "giving employment" have been very muddled. But taking our spending as a whole, it has bcon fairly legitimate. - Conditions, however, have altogether altered, not alone for the present, but for years,to come, and we must now look at the question from a new and entirely differ- ent standpoint. Those who by their continued demand keep labour in unnecessary channels, reduce the possible productive wealth by which the country is sustained, and by its continued reproduction we can alone conquer. On the other hand those who resolutely limit their ex- penditure to. those things thab aro necessary to maintain mental and physical efficiency are doing their Fluty to their country in her hour of need. Upon the extent that the call to save is responded to by all classes of the community

depends the duration of the war, and in a largo measure its final issue. CHARLES E. PARKER.

LORD DERBY WAR HOSPITAL

On Saturday the St. Helens Amateur ()per= atic Society paid their second visit to the above hospital and gave an excellent Concert, which was greatly appreciated by the patients. . His Worship the Mayor lAlderman Peter Peacock). chairman of the Executive Committee, on their behalf., begs to remind the Public that the PARR HALL Ls NOW OPEN between the hours of 10 a.m. and 4 p.m. to receive their GIFTS to the above funds in the shape of Furniture, Pictures, Curios. Useful and Ornamental Articles, or any- thing that could be sold by auction a.nd oonverted i into cash; of which there s imperative need. Most people have articles laid aside for which they have no use; some have articles of value they could well spare. A direct appeal is made to all persons to contribute. PRIVATE WALTER HOLMES, Wounded and Missing.

wounded and missing, -and since then nothing has been seen. Or heard of him. His wife, who lives at 55, Hume-street, would be glad if any- body who may come in contact with him would communicate With her. Private 'Holmes, who is a native of Manches- ter, formerly belonged to the Manchester Vol- unteers.. He came to Warrington about three years ago, and at the outbreak of .war he joined the Warrington'. Territorial's. He had pre-, v iously been employed by Messrs. Armitage and Rigby. took ,interest in football and bowls. • He is twenty-seven years of age.. , PRIVATE ROBERT TAYLOR. • Wounded -And Missing For Over Two Months. .
